\displaystyle=N^{~}_{G}+c_{1}(\mathfrak{F})+\frac{1}{2}\left(c_{1}^{2}(\mathfrak{F})-2c^{~}_{2}(\mathfrak{F})\right)+\frac{1}{6}\left(c_{1}^{3}(\mathfrak{F})-3c_{1}(\mathfrak{F})\hskip 1.99997ptc^{~}_{2}(\mathfrak{F})+3c_{3}(\mathfrak{F})\right)+\cdots,

where
c_{j}(\mathfrak{F})
is the
j
’th Chern class, and
N^{~}_{G}
is a dimension of the structure group.
The first three terms may appear for the four-dimensional space.

Definition 2.9

(
\hat{A}
-genus)
.

\hat{A}
-genera are defined as follows:

1.

\hat{A}
-genus in Lie algebra:

Suppose
\{{\pm}i\lambda_{1},\cdots,{\pm}i\lambda_{k}\}
are eigenvalues of
F\in\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(2k,\mathbb{R})
with
F+F^{T}=0
; namely askew-symmetric matrix.

\hat{A}
-genus is defined such that:

\displaystyle\hat{A}(F):=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{-\lambda_{j}/4\pi}{\sinh{\left(-\lambda_{j}/4\pi\right)}}.

2.

\hat{A}
-genus in fibre-bundle:

When
\mathfrak{F}\in\Omega^{2}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
is a curvature two-form of a bundle with structure group
G
and its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,
\hat{A}
-genus
\hat{A}(\mathfrak{F})\in{\mathfrak{W}}(\mathfrak{F})
is defined such that:

\displaystyle\hat{A}(M):=\mathrm{det}^{1/2}\left(\frac{-i\mathfrak{F}/4\pi}{\sinh{\left(-i\mathfrak{F}/4\pi\right)}}\right).

\hat{A}
-genus is sometimes referred to as the Dirac-genus in physics
[
24
]
.
The first several terms of
\hat{A}
-genus in manifold
M
is provided such that:

\displaystyle\hat{A}(M)=1-\frac{1}{24}p_{1}(M)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5760}\left(7\hskip 1.00006ptp^{2}_{1}(M)-4\hskip 1.00006ptp_{2}(M)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{967680}\left(-31\hskip 1.00006ptp_{1}^{3}(M)+44\hskip 1.00006ptp_{1}(M)\hskip 1.00006ptp_{2}(M)-16\hskip 1.00006ptp_{3}(M)\right)+\cdots,

where
p_{j}(M)
is the
j^{\textrm{th}}
Pontrjagin class in an even-dimensional manifold
M
.
The first three terms may appear for the four-dimensional space.

Spin manifold:

Suppose a spin structure is defined in
n
-dimensional smooth and oriented manifold
M
, where
n
is even integer.
It is denoted by
Spin(M)
and Clifford algebra of
Spin(M)
is denoted as
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(M)
.
Manifold
M
is lifted to a spin manifold owing to Clifford module
{\mathcal{}S}:={\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(M)\otimes\mathbb{C}
, and spinor group is doubly covering
M
and induces spinor bundle of
\left({\mathcal{}S},\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},M,Spin(M)\right)
.
Clifford algebra is introduced in base manifold
M
:
A representation of
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(M)
is denoted as
\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}

(a=0,\cdots,n-1)
yielding

\displaystyle\left\{\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a},\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{b}\right\}

\displaystyle:=\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}\times_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{b}+\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{b}\times_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}~=~2\eta^{ab}_{~}\hskip 1.99997pt{{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},

(67)

where
{{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is a unit Clifford algebra, and
\times_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
represents Clifford product.
Hereafter. Clifford product
\times_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is omitted for simplicity.
Although each component can be set as Hermitian self-conjugate or anti-self-conjugate, all of them cannot be set entirely self-conjugate nor anti-self-conjugate, when manifold
M
is non-compact.
This study utilizes a representation such that

\displaystyle(\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0})^{\dagger}=\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0},

\displaystyle~~(\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{i})^{\dagger}=-\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{i},~\textrm{for}~~i=1,\cdots,n-1.

(68)

We note that a relation
(\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a})^{\dagger}=\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}
follows from (
67
) and (
68
) for any
a{\leq}n-1
.
Representation (
68
) is always possible independent from the chosen basis.
The chiral operator and the projection operator are defined, respectively, as

\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}:=i\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}^{0}\cdots\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}^{n-1},~~\textrm{and}~~P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:=\frac{{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\pm\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}{2};

they are independent from chosen basis owing to their definition.
The chiral operator fulfils relations:

\displaystyle({\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}})^{2}={\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},

\displaystyle~~~{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}=-\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}},

(69)

for any
a{\leq}n-1
.

Index theorem:

The elliptic-type Dirac-operator in a compact space has an analytic index equivalent to the topological index owing to the Atiyah– Singer theorem.
We introduce the index of the Dirac operator as follows:
Suppose
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}:(M,{\mathcal{}S})\rightarrow(M,{\mathcal{}S})
is a self-adjoint Dirac operator.
Clifford algebra
\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading space in the spinor module as
{\mathcal{}S}={\mathcal{}S}^{+}\oplus{\mathcal{}S}^{-}
owing the chiral operator
\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
such that:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{}S}^{\pm}:=\{\psi\in{\mathcal{}S}|\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\psi=\pm\psi\},

where
\psi\in{\mathcal{}S}
is a spinor field defined as a section in
{T\hskip-1.99997ptM}
.
Restriction of
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}
acting on
{\mathcal{}S}^{\pm}
are defined as
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{\pm}:={\not\!\mathcal{}D}P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}
.
Spinor fields are divided into two parts as
\psi^{\pm}:=P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}\psi\in{\mathcal{}S}^{\pm}
.
An index of the Dirac operator and a super trace are introduced as follows:

Definition 2.10

(Index of the Dirac operator
[
16
]
)
.

An index of Dirac operator
{\mathcal{}D}
is defined as

\displaystyle ind({\not\!\mathcal{}D})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{dim}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathrm{Ker}\left({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}\right)-\mathrm{dim}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathrm{Ker}\left({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}\right),

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathrm{Ker}\left({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}\right)+\mathrm{dim}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathrm{Coker}\left({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}\right).

The heat kernel, denoted as
\mathcal{H}_{s}
, is the unique operator that satisfies the equation such that:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{2}\right)\mathcal{H}_{s}

\displaystyle=0.

The McKean–Singer theorem
[
25
]
states a relation between a heat kernel and an index of the Dirac operator as follows:

Remark 2.11

(McKean–Singer)
.

Suppose
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}
is a Dirac operator on a compact space and
e^{-s\hskip 0.81949pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{\mp}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{\pm}}\in\mathcal{H}_{s}
.
The index of
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}
is provided as

\displaystyle ind({\not\!\mathcal{}D})=Str\left(e^{-s\hskip 0.81949pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[e^{-s\hskip 0.81949pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}}\right]-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[e^{-s\hskip 0.81949pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}}\right]={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\Gamma\hskip 1.99997pte^{-s\hskip 0.81949pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{2}}\right].

Proof.

When
H^{\pm}_{\lambda}
are eigenspaces of
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{\mp}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{\pm}
with an eigenvalue
\lambda
, and
n^{\pm}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{dim}H^{\pm}_{\lambda}
, it is provided that:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[e^{-s\hskip 0.81949pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}}\right]-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[e^{-s\hskip 0.81949pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}}\right]=\sum_{\lambda\geq 0}\left(n^{+}_{\lambda}-n^{-}_{\lambda}\right)e^{-s\lambda}.

(70)

When
\lambda\neq 0
, operator
\lambda^{-1}\circ{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}\circ{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}:H^{+}_{\lambda}\rightarrow H^{+}_{\lambda}
is an identity map; thus,
n^{+}_{\lambda}-n^{-}_{\lambda}=0
.
Therefore, it is is maintained that

(\ref{nmn})=n^{+}_{0}-n^{-}_{0}=ind({\not\!\mathcal{}D}).

Moreover, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}

\displaystyle={\not\!\mathcal{}D}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}={\mathcal{}D}{\mathcal{}D}(P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{+}-P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{-})=({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}+{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-})({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}-{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}),

\displaystyle={\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}-{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-},

using relations
\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{+}-P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{-}
,
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}={\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}+{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}
and

{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{\pm}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{\pm}={\not\!\mathcal{}D}P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}={\not\!\mathcal{}D}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\mp}P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}=0.

Thus, it is provided that

\displaystyle\Gamma({\not\!\mathcal{}D}{\not\!\mathcal{}D})^{n}

\displaystyle=\left({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}\right)^{n}-\left({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+}{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{-}\right)^{n}.

Therefore, the second equality is maintained.
∎

Remark 2.12

(Atiyah–Singer
[
2
]
)
.

Suppose
M
is a compact and oriented even-dimensional spin manifold with no boundary,
{\mathcal{}S}
is a Clifford module.

1.

For Dirac operator
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}
in
\Gamma(M,{\mathcal{}S})
, an index of
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}
is provided such that:

\displaystyle ind({\not\!\mathcal{}D})=\int_{M}\hat{A}(M),

(71)

when
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}
is the Fredholm operator.

2.

Suppose
{\mathcal{}W}
is a vector bundle in
M
.
For twisting Dirac operator
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{\mathcal{}W}
in
\Gamma(M,{\mathcal{}W}\otimes{\mathcal{}S})
, an index of
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{\mathcal{}W}
is provided such that:

\displaystyle ind({\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{\mathcal{}W})=\int_{M}\hat{A}(M)ch({\mathcal{}W}),

(72)

when
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{\mathcal{}W}
is the Fredholm operator.

A proof of the theorem is given in literatures, e.g., chapter 4 in Ref.
[
26
]
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{R}
is a curvature of four-dimensional manifold
M_{4}
and
\mathfrak{F}^{I}\tau^{~}_{I}
is a curvature of principal bundle
{\mathcal{}W}
with structure group
G
whose Lie algebra is
\tau^{~}_{I}\in\mathfrak{g}
.
The index is provided as

\displaystyle ind({\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{\mathcal{}W})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{M_{4}}\left(\frac{n}{48}{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{{T\hskip-1.63885ptM}}[\mathfrak{R}\wedge\mathfrak{R}]-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{G}[\mathfrak{F}\wedge\mathfrak{F}]\right),

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}\int_{M_{4}}\left(\frac{n}{12}\eta^{~}_{\circ\circ}\eta^{~}_{\star\star}\mathfrak{R}^{{\circ}\star}\wedge\mathfrak{R}^{{\circ}\star}+i\sum_{I=1}^{n}f^{I}_{~JK}\mathfrak{F}^{J}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{K}\right).

(73)

We note that
{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{{T\hskip-1.63885ptM}}[\mathfrak{R}]=\eta_{\circ\circ}\mathfrak{R}^{\circ\circ}=0
due to anti-symmetry of
\mathfrak{R}
with respect to its tensor indices.
The index in the
\theta
-metric space is provided by replacing
{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}\rightarrow{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{~}_{\theta}
.

2.5.2
Cobordism

René Tom has proposed a method to categorise compact and orientable
C^{\infty}
-manifolds into the equivalence classes named “
cobordant
[
27
,
28
]
” in 1953.
When a disjoint union of two
n
-dimensional manifolds is the boundary of a compact
(n+1)
-dimensional manifold, they are cobordant to each other; the cobordant is an equivalence relation and quotient space

\displaystyle\Omega_{C}^{n}:=\{n\textrm{-dimensional~closed~manifolds}\}/\hskip-1.99997pt\sim

forms an abelian group, where
\sim
is an equivalent relation with respect to cobordism.
We present a definition and elementary remarks of cobordism in this section.
For details of the cobordism theory, see, e.g., Chapter 7 in Ref.
[
29
]
.

Definition 2.13

(cobordant)
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are compact and oriented
n
-dimensional smooth manifolds, and
\omega_{\bullet}
denotes an orientation of oriented manifold
\bullet
.

(M,\omega_{M})
and
(N,\omega_{N})
are called “cobordant” to each other, if there exists a compact and oriented
(n\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.99997pt1)
-dimensional smooth manifold
(W,\omega_{W})
such that

\displaystyle(\partial{W},\hskip 1.99997pt\partial{\omega_{W}})

\displaystyle=(M,-\omega_{M})\amalg(N,\omega_{N}),

and
W
is called a cobordism from
M
to
N
.
When
M
and
N
are cobordant to each other owing to
W
, it is denoted as
M\sim_{W}N
in this study.
Cobordant induces an equivalent class among compact and oriented
n
-dimensional smooth manifolds, namely, a cobordant class denoted by
\Omega_{C}^{n}
.

Remark 2.14
.

Cobordant class
\Omega_{C}^{n}
forms the abelian group concerning the disjoint-union operation.

Proof.

When map
M_{0}\rightarrow M_{1}
is diffeomorphic,
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are cobordant; thus,
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are cobordant with respect to closed period
\theta=[0,1]
;
M_{0}\sim_{M_{0}\otimes[0,1]}M_{1}
such that

\partial{(M_{\theta}\otimes[0,1])}=(M_{0}\otimes 0)\amalg(M_{1}\otimes 1)

with appropriate orientations.
It fulfils the associative and commutative laws trivially.
Any compact manifold
V=\partial{W}
is the zero element owing to
(M\amalg V)\sim_{W}M
.
The inverse of
(M,\omega_{M})
is given by
(M,-\omega_{M})
.
Therefore, the remark is maintained.
∎

Remark 2.15

(Pontrjagin)
.

Characteristic numbers are cobordism invariant.

Proof.

A proof is provided for the Pontrjagin number; it is sufficient to show that the Pontrjagin numbers on
M
vanish when
M=\partial W
.
There exists one-dimensional trivial bundle
\xi
such that
TW\bigl|_{M}=TM\oplus\xi
; thus, for any inclusion map
\iota:M\hookrightarrow{W}
, relation
p_{i}(M)=\iota^{\#}(p_{i}(W))
is fulfilled.
Therefore, for any invariant polynomial
f\in{\mathfrak{W}}(M)
to define characteristic numbers, it is provided such that:

\displaystyle f\left(p_{1},p_{2},\cdots\right)[M]=\int_{M}f\left(p\left(M\right)\right)=\int_{\partial M}\iota^{\#}\left(f\left(p\left(W\right)\right)\right)=\int_{W}df\left(p\left(W\right)\right)=0,

owing to the Stokes theorem; thus, the lemma is maintained.
Here,
[\bullet]
denotes a cobordism class of manifold
M
.
∎

2.6
\theta
-metric space and index theorem

The Atiyah–Singer topological index is defined for the elliptic-type Dirac operator.
On the other hand, the Dirac operator in the Minkowski space has a hyperbolic type; thus, the theorem is not fulfilled as its original shape.
Gibbons has discussed an analytic continuation of functions defined in Eudlidean space to that in the Minkowski space in a study of a Fermion number violation in curved space-time
[
30
,
31
]
; he pointed out in Ref.
[
30
]
that passing from Eudlidean to the Minkowski space is problematic as only non-singular manifolds admit an analytic continuation to real Riemannian space.
On the other hand, Bär and Strohmaier have provided the index theorem for a globally hyperbolic manifold with boundaries in a mathematically rigorous manner and applied the theorem to the total charge generation due to the gravitational and gauge field background
[
32
]
.

This section discusses the Atiyah–Singer topological index in the space-time manifold with no boundary using the cobordism theory with the
\theta
-metric.
Suppose
{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}
is a four-dimensional compact and oriented manifold with no boundary associating a standard Euclidean topological phase.
Two manifolds
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}:={\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow 0}
and
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}:={\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow 1}
are cobordant with respect to five-dimensional manifold
W_{\theta}:={{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}}\otimes(\theta\in[0,1])
; manifold
W_{\theta}
is cobordism from Lorentzian manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
to Euclidean manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
(see the upper half of Figure
2
).

Figure 2:

Schematic drowning of five-dimensional manifolds
W_{\theta}
and
W^{\prime}_{\theta}
, and its boundary manifolds.
Four-dimensional boundary manifolds
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{}M}_{-1}
have a Euclidean metric, and
{\mathcal{}M}_{-1}
has a Minkowski metric.
Real (solid line) and imaginary (dashed line) parts of
e^{i\pi\theta/2}
are show in the figure.

A representation of Clifford algebra in
Spin({\mathcal{}M}_{\theta})
is defined to fulfil
algebra

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{\theta}^{a},\gamma_{\theta}^{b}\}=2\eta_{\theta}^{ab}{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}

(74)

with any values of
\theta
as follows:

\displaystyle\gamma^{0}_{\theta}:=e^{i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{0}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},

\displaystyle~~\textrm{and}~~\gamma^{j}_{\theta}:=\gamma^{j}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}~\textrm{for}~~j=1,2,3.

(75)

Here,
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\theta}
is equivalent to
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
at
\theta=0
, and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{E}:={\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\theta}|_{\theta\rightarrow 1-0}
is the Clifford algebra in Eudlidean space
(See lower half of Figure
2
).
Clifford algebra
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{E}
is Hermitian anti-self-conjugate owing to (
68
).
Accordingly, the chiral- and projection-operators are defined as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}:=\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}\big|_{\gamma_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}\rightarrow\gamma_{\theta}}~~\textrm{and}~~P^{\pm}_{\theta}:=P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\big|_{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}\rightarrow\Gamma_{\theta}},

respectively.

A
(4\hskip-1.00006pt\times\hskip-1.00006pt4)
-matrix representation using Clifford algebra
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\theta}
is introduced in the
\theta
-metric space.
A point
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}}}:=(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})\in{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}
is represented using a
(4\hskip-1.00006pt\times\hskip-1.00006pt4)
-matrix representation as

q

\displaystyle:={{\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}}}\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\theta}=\sum_{a=0}^{3}\xi^{a}\gamma_{\theta}^{a},

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&e^{i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006ptx^{0}-x^{3}&-x^{1}+ix^{2}\\
0&0&-x^{1}-ix^{2}&e^{i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006ptx^{0}+x^{3}\\
e^{i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006ptx^{0}+x^{3}&x^{1}-ix^{2}&0&0\\
x^{1}+ix^{2}&e^{i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006ptx^{0}-x^{3}&0&0\\
\end{array}\right),

where the chiral representation of
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is used.
A generator of a
rotational
transformation in the
\theta
-metric space is provided as

\displaystyle\Lambda^{~}_{\theta}:=\sum^{~}_{a<b}c^{~}_{ab}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{a}_{\theta}\gamma^{b}_{\theta},

where
c^{~}_{ab}\in\mathbb{R}
is six real-parameters of
rotational angles
; determinant of
\Lambda_{\theta}
normalized to unity.
This
rotation
preserves a determinant as

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}\left[\Lambda^{~}_{\theta}{{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}}\Lambda_{\theta}^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}[{{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}}]=\left(e^{i\pi\theta}t^{2}-x^{2}-y^{2}-z^{2}\right)^{2}=\left(\eta^{~}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\xi^{\circ}\xi^{\circ}\right)^{2},

and it is real valued at
\theta=0
and
\theta=1
.

An index of the Dirac operator in the Minkowski space is defined owing to cobordism in the
\theta
-metric space:

Definition 2.16
.

Suppose
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}}
is the twisting Dirac operator in
\Gamma({\mathcal{}M}_{\theta},{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}\otimes{\mathcal{}S}_{\theta})
and the spin-connection
{\mathfrak{w}}
and principal connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{G}=[{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{G}]_{\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\tau^{~}_{I}
with structure group
G
exist in
{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\neq 0}
.

{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}
has topological indices provided by a cohomology formula such as

\displaystyle ind(i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{0}):=ind(i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{1})=\int_{{\mathcal{}M}_{1}}\hat{A}({\mathcal{}M}_{1})ch({\mathcal{}W}_{1}),

where
{\mathcal{}W}_{1}:=\left.{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}\right|_{\theta\rightarrow 1-0}
.

Theorem 2.17
.

Definition
2.16
is well-defined.

Proof.

Owing to the assumption,
{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}
is a four-dimensional compact and oriented manifold with no boundary, and
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
is Eudlidean manifold.
Differential operator
i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{1}
is the Hermitian anti-self-conjugate Dirac operator concerning Clifford algebra
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{~}_{E}
given above.
A Laplacian concerning
{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}}
is provided such that:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\theta}:={{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}}{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{a}_{\theta}\gamma^{b}_{\theta}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}=\left(2\eta_{\theta}^{ab}-\gamma^{b}_{\theta}\gamma^{a}_{\theta}\right)\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b},

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}}{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}}

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\theta}(\nabla_{0})^{2}-(\nabla_{1})^{2}-(\nabla_{2})^{2}-(\nabla_{3})^{2},

where

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{a}-i\frac{1}{2}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\eta_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\eta_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\star\star}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathcal{E}}^{\mu}_{a}\omega^{~{\circ}\star}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{{S}}^{{\circ}\star}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}-i{c^{~}_{G}}\hskip 1.00006pt\left[{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{G}\right]_{a}\tau^{~}_{I},

where
{S}^{\bullet\bullet}_{\theta}
is a generator of a
Spin(1,3)
group in the
\theta
-metric space and represented as

\displaystyle{S}^{ab}_{\theta}

\displaystyle:=i[\gamma_{\theta}^{a},\gamma_{\theta}^{b}]/4

using the trivial basis.
Thus, a Laplacian is elliptic operator when
\theta=1
and Atiyah–Singer index is well-defined in
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
.
On the other hand,
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
are cobordant such that
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}\sim_{[0,1]\otimes{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}}{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
and connections exists in
{\mathcal{}W}_{\theta}
with
\theta\in(0,1]
; thus,
{\mathcal{}M}_{+0}
and
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
are homotopically equivalent.
Therefore, Pontrjagin and Chern classes are preserved owing to
Remark
2.15
.
The Atiyah–Singer index in hyperbolic space is defined as a corresponding index in
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
;
ind(i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{0}):=ind(i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{1})
.
Therefore, the Atiyah–Singer index in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}_{1}
is well-defined.
∎

The same result is obtained owing to cobordism
{\mathcal{}M}_{-1}\sim_{W^{\prime}_{\theta}}{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
where
W^{\prime}_{\theta}:=[-1,0]\otimes{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}
and gives
ind(i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{0}):=ind(i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{-1})=ind(i{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{1})
.
When
\gamma_{E}^{0}=\gamma_{\theta}^{0}|_{\theta\rightarrow 1+0}
is replaced by
-\gamma_{E}^{0}=\gamma_{\theta}^{0}|_{\theta\rightarrow 1-0}
in Eudlidean space-time, the Clifford algebra (
74
) does not change.

3
Principal bundles

This section introduces several principal bundles necessary to construct the Yang–Mills theory.
Structure groups, namely the gauge-group, of principal bundles govern phenomena induced by a connection and a curvature of the Yang–Mills theory through an equation of motion.
The theory of principal bundles with the structural gauge group provides the Yang–Mills theory’s backbone.
Physically, matter fields are spinor sections of the principal gauge bundle, and forces, including a gravitational force, are represented owing to connections and curvatures.

This section discusses principal bundles mainly with the Lorentzian metric for simplicity; the determinant of the metric is explicitly indicated if necessary.
Extension to the
\theta
-metric is realized by replacing
{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}\mapsto{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}_{\theta}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$\epsilon$}}\mapsto{\mbox{\boldmath$\epsilon$}}_{\theta}
and
{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\eta}}}\mapsto{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}}
according to (
18
).
We note that the metric tensor and its inverse are hidden in the index-raising and -lowering operations.

3.1
Principal bundles of space-time manifold

A principal bundle is a tuple such that
(E,\pi,M,G)
, where
E
and
M
are total and base spaces, respectively, and
\pi:E\rightarrow M
is a projection (bundle) map, and
G
is an associating structure group.
A group operator acts simply transitively from the right
3
3

3

A group operator of the co-Poincaré group is defined exceptionally acting from the left.
.
A duplex superspace appears in the Yang–Mills theory and general relativity in four-dimensions as introduced in section
2.4.2
.

3.1.1
Co-Poincaré bundle

The Yang–Mills theory has the Poincaré symmetry in addition to the gauge-symmetry.
On the other hand, general relativity is not invariant under the four-dimensional translation.
The author introduces a modified translation operator to preserve in general relativity
[
33
,
34
]
.
This section discusses a principal bundle in the space-time manifold with the structure co-Poincaré group.

We extend a structure group of the inertial bundle to Poincaré group:

\displaystyle I\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO(1,3)=SO(1,3)\ltimes T^{4}.

Representations of Lie algebra
\mathfrak{i}{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(1,3)
of Poincaré group is obtained using the trivial basis as follows
[
35
]
:

\displaystyle\left[P_{a},P_{b}\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\left[J_{ab},P_{c}\right]

\displaystyle=-\eta_{ac}P_{b}+\eta_{bc}P_{a},

(76)

\displaystyle\left[J_{ab},J_{cd}\right]

\displaystyle=-\eta_{ac}J_{bd}+\eta_{bc}J_{ad}-\eta_{bd}J_{ac}+\eta_{ad}J_{bc}.

where
P_{a}
and
J_{ab}
are generators of the
T^{4}
group and the
SO(1,3)
group, respectively.

The Einstein–Hilbert gravitational Lagrangian does not respect the Poincaé symmetry
[
36
]
.
We introduce the co-Poincaré symmetry extending the Poincaé symmetry here.
The co-Poincaré symmetry, which is denoted as
G_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}
, is the symmetry in which the translation operator is replaced by the co-translation operator.
A generator of the co-translation is defined as
P_{ab}:=P_{a}\iota_{b}
, where
\iota_{b}
is a contraction with respect to trivial frame field
\partial_{b}
.
Lie algebra of the co-Poincaré group are provided as

\displaystyle\left[P_{ab},P_{cd}\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\left[J_{ab},P_{cd}\right]

\displaystyle=-\eta_{ac}P_{bd}+\eta_{bc}P_{ad},

and
\left[J_{ab},J_{cd}\right]
in (
76
).
The structure constants of the co-Poincaré group can be obtained from above Lie algebra through a relation,
[\Theta_{I},\Theta_{J}]:={\mathcal{F}}_{~IJ}^{K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Theta_{K}
, where
\left[\Theta_{I}\right]_{ab;cd}:=\left(\left[\Theta_{1}\right]_{ab},\left[\Theta_{2}\right]_{cd}\right)=\left(P_{ab},J_{cd}\right)
.
Lie algebra of the co-Poincaré symmetry is denoted as
\mathfrak{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}
.
Each component of the structure constant is provided by direct calculation using the trivial basis as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathcal{F}}_{~11}^{1}={\mathcal{F}}_{~11}^{2}={\mathcal{F}}_{~12}^{2}={\mathcal{F}}_{~21}^{2}={\mathcal{F}}_{~22}^{1}=0,\\
\left[{\mathcal{F}}_{~12}^{1}\right]_{ab;cd}^{ef}=-\left[{\mathcal{F}}_{~21}^{1}\right]_{cd;ab}^{ef}=\eta_{ac}\delta^{e}_{b}\delta^{f}_{d}-\eta_{bc}\delta^{e}_{a}\delta^{f}_{d},\\
\left[{\mathcal{F}}_{~22}^{2}\right]_{ab;cd}^{ef}=-\eta_{ac}\delta_{b}^{e}\delta_{d}^{f}+\eta_{bc}\delta_{a}^{e}\delta_{d}^{f}-\eta_{bd}\delta_{a}^{e}\delta_{c}^{f}+\eta_{ad}\delta_{b}^{e}\delta_{c}^{f}.\end{array}\right.

Connection form
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}
and curvature form
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}
are, respectively, introduced as Lie-algebra valued one- and two-forms concerning co-Poincaré group, and they are expressed using the trivial basis as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle=J_{\circ\circ}\otimes{\mathfrak{w}}^{\circ\circ}+P_{\circ\circ}\otimes\hskip 11.99998pt{\mathfrak{S}}^{\circ\circ}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}\otimes{\Omega}^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle=J_{\circ\circ}\otimes\mathfrak{R}^{\circ\circ}+P_{\circ\circ}\otimes d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{S}}^{\circ\circ}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}\otimes{\Omega}^{2}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}).

We note that
P^{\circ\circ}\otimes d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\circ\circ}\in\Omega^{2}
due to
\iota_{\bullet}:\Omega^{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{p-1}
, e.g.,
\iota_{a}{\mathfrak{e}}^{b}=\delta^{b}_{a}
.
A principal co-Poincaré bundle is defined as follows:

Definition 3.1

(principal co-Poincaré bundle)
.

A principal co-Poincaré bundle is a tuple
\left({\mathcal{}M},\pi_{\mathrm{I}},\mathscr{M},G_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}\right)
,
where a co-Poincaré group operator acts on the the total space from the left.

3.1.2
Duplex superspace in general relativity

The Hodge-dual operator works as the parity operator and induces a duplex superspace in
\Omega^{2}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}})
over the four-dimensional manifold as shown in section
2.4.2
.
The superspace consisting of curvature two-forms of the inertial bundle is crucial in physics because it is related to the structure of the space-time itself.
Various representations of general relativity in four-dimensions are summarized in Ref.
[
37
]
.

Definition 3.2

(Hodge-dual Lorentz curvature)
.

A Hodge-dual of the curvature of the inertial bundle is defined owing to the Hodge-dual operator as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}:\mathfrak{R}^{ab}\mapsto\hat{\mathfrak{R}}^{ab}:=\frac{1}{2}\hat{R}^{ab}_{\hskip 6.5556pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ},

where

\displaystyle\hat{R}^{ab}_{\hskip 6.5556ptcd}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sqrt{{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\eta}}}}}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{R}^{ab}_{\hskip 6.5556pt{\star\star}}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{{\circ}\star}\eta^{{\circ}\star}\hskip 1.00006pt\epsilon_{{\circ\circ}cd}.

The Hodge-dual operator squared yields eigenvalue equation
{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}{\circ}{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathfrak{R}^{ab})=\mathfrak{R}^{ab}
; thus, the operator has eigenvalues of
\pm 1
.
Owing to
Remark
2.6
, a space of the two-form objects is split into two subspaces as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}:=P_{\textrm{H}}^{\pm}(\mathfrak{R})\in V^{\pm}~~\textrm{yielding}~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathfrak{R}^{\pm})=\pm\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}.

Surface form
{\mathfrak{S}}\in V^{2}({T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}})\otimes\Omega^{2}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}})\otimes{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(1,3)
is expressed using tensor coefficients as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{ab}=\frac{1}{2}S^{ab}_{\hskip 5.7361pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ},~~\textrm{where}~~S^{ab}_{\hskip 5.7361ptcd}=\epsilon^{ab}_{\hskip 5.7361ptcd}.

Any two-form objects are represented using the parity bases as shown in (
47
); thus, the surface form can be expressed using a sum of two eigenstates of the Hodge-dual operator as
{\mathfrak{S}}={\mathfrak{S}}^{+}+{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}
.

Curvature and surface forms in the inertial bundle make the Kramers pairs
{K\hskip-1.99997ptP}(\mathfrak{R})
and
{K\hskip-1.99997ptP}({\mathfrak{S}})
.
For the Kramers pair of the spin-connection
{K\hskip-1.99997ptP}({\mathfrak{w}})
,
\hat{\mathfrak{w}}
is provided as a solution of the structure equation:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{R}}

\displaystyle=d\hat{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\mathfrak{w}}\wedge\hat{\mathfrak{w}},

for given
\hat{\mathfrak{R}}
.

We construct the secondary superspace in the inertial bundle.
The covariant differential in the Lorentzian manifold induces the Dirac operator such that:

\displaystyle{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{~}_{\mathfrak{w}}

\displaystyle:=\gamma{\mathcal{}D}^{~}_{\mathfrak{w}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&d+{{{c}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}[\hat{\mathfrak{w}},]_{\wedge}\\
d+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}[{\mathfrak{w}},]_{\wedge}&0\end{array}\right),

where
{\mathcal{}D}^{~}_{\mathfrak{w}}:=
diag
(d^{~}_{{\mathfrak{w}}},\hat{d}^{~}_{{\mathfrak{w}}})
.
The Lorentz curvature is represented in the secondary superspace as

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathfrak{R}$}}^{~}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathfrak{R}&0\\
0&\hat{\mathfrak{R}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}d\left[{\mathfrak{w}}\right]^{ab}+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}\left[{\mathfrak{w}}\right]^{a}_{~{\circ}}\wedge\left[{\mathfrak{w}}\right]^{{\circ}b}&0\\
0&d\left[\hat{\mathfrak{w}}\right]^{ab}+{{{c}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}\left[\hat{\mathfrak{w}}\right]^{a}_{~{\circ}}\wedge\left[\hat{\mathfrak{w}}\right]^{{\circ}b}\end{array}\right).

The second Bianchi identity is represented as

\displaystyle{\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{~}_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathfrak{R}$}}^{~}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&d\hat{\mathfrak{R}}+{{{c}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}[\hat{\mathfrak{w}},\hat{\mathfrak{R}}]_{\wedge}\\
d\mathfrak{R}+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}[{\mathfrak{w}},\mathfrak{R}]_{\wedge}&0\end{array}\right)={\mbox{\boldmath$0$}}

in the secondary superspace.

Dual connection and curvature are also provided in the co-Poincaré bundle as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle=J_{\circ\circ}\otimes\hat{\mathfrak{w}}^{\circ\circ}+P_{\circ\circ}\otimes\hskip 11.99998pt\hat{\mathfrak{S}}^{\circ\circ}

\displaystyle\in\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}\otimes{\Omega}^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}),

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle=J_{\circ\circ}\otimes\hat{\mathfrak{R}}^{\circ\circ}+P_{\circ\circ}\otimes\hat{d}^{~}_{{\mathfrak{w}}}\hat{\mathfrak{S}}^{\circ\circ}

\displaystyle\in\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}\otimes{\Omega}^{2}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}).

The gravitational form is introduced in the secondary superspace as follows:

Definition 3.3

(gravitational form)
.

The gravitational form is defined owing to the co-Poincaré curvature in the secondary superspace such that:

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathfrak{F}$}}^{~}_{Gr}

\displaystyle:=\gamma\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}&0\\
0&\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}\\
\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptc\hskip-0.40974pt\mathrm{P}}&0\end{array}\right).

The Einstein–Hilbert gravitational Lagrangian is represented using the gravitational form.

3.2
Principal bundles in Yang–Mills theory

The Yang–Mills theory is constructed on the inertial manifold.
A spinor section describing a physical matter field is introduced in the spin manifold over
{\mathcal{}M}
.
The matter field is an element of a compact Lie group, namely a gauge group.
A connection and curvature of the principal bundle are also physical objects which mediate a
force
among matter fields.

Two structure groups,
Spin(M)
and
SU(N)
, are introduced; the former concerns symmetry in the Lorentzian manifold, the latter is an internal symmetry group and governing property of a gauge interaction (gauge force).
These bundles induce a superspace, and they are twisted into each other.
This section introduces principal bundles, their connections and curvatures appearing in the Yang–Mills theory.

3.2.1
Spinor bundle

A spin structure is introduced in
{\mathcal{}M}
through spinor group
Spin(V)
, where
V
is a vector space over
{\mathcal{}M}
.

Spin(V)
is also denoted as
Spin(1,3)
when
V
has the
SO(1,3)
symmetry.
It is known that a
Spin(1,3)
group doubly covers a
SO(1,3)
group such that
Spin(1,3)\cong SO(1,3)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}U(1)
under covering map
\tau_{\hskip-0.81949ptcov}:Spin(1,3)\rightarrow SO(1,3)\otimes\{\pm 1\}
.
A Clifford algebra of
Spin(1,3)
is denoted as
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(1,3)
.
When a spin structure is defined globally in
{\mathcal{}M}
, it is denoted
Spin({\mathcal{}M})
, and the inertial manifold is extended to a spin manifold.

Definition 3.4

(Principal spinor bundle)
.

The principal spinor bundle is a tuple as follows:

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},{\mathcal{}M},Spin(1,3)\right).

A total space is Clifford module
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}={\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C}
.
Projection map
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is provided owing to the covering map as follows:

\displaystyle\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:=\tau_{\hskip-0.81949ptcov}/\{\pm 1\}:{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes{\mathcal{}M}\rightarrow{\mathcal{}M}/\{\pm 1\}:\left.\psi\right|_{p}\hskip 1.00006pt\mapsto p\in{\mathcal{}M},

where
\psi\in\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)
.
The structure group of the inertial bundle,
SO(1,3)
, is lifted to
Spin(1,3)
owing to projection map
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
; a spin structure is induced globally in
{\mathcal{}M}
.

We assume the existence of a spin structure globally; a spinor is a section in
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
.
The Lorentz transformation of a spinor is provided as follows:

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}:{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes{\mathcal{}M}\rightarrow{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:\psi(x)\mapsto S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})\psi(x).

Vector
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{a}\partial_{a}\in{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}\otimes{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is a Clifford algebra valued vector in
{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
transformed as an adjoint representation under the Lorentz transformation such that:

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}({\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})

\displaystyle:=S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}){\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}={\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1}.

A spinor product of spinor and Clifford algebra are compatible with the Lorentz transformation as follows:

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}:{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\psi(p)\mapsto\left(S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}){\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}\right)\left(S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})\psi(\Lambda p)\right)=S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\psi(\Lambda p)\right)\cong{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\psi(p).

Vector space
V_{\psi}
has
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading structure
V_{\psi}=V_{\psi}^{+}\oplus V_{\psi}^{-}
, where

\displaystyle V_{\psi}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\psi^{\pm}\in V_{\psi}|{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\psi^{\pm}=\pm\psi^{\pm}\right\}.

Any spinors belong to one of two chiral sets
V_{\psi}^{\pm}
according to an eigenvalue of the chiral operator.
Chiral projection operator
P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}:V_{\psi}\rightarrow V_{\psi}
acts on a spinor as
P_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}\psi=\psi^{\pm}\in V_{\psi}^{\pm}
.

A representation of Clifford module
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}({\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C})
can be expressed using a direct sum of four isomorphic representations.
Suppose
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}
is a subspace spanned by Clifford products of an even number of elements.
In this case, a representation of
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}
is provided using a direct sum of two irreducible representations,
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}({\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C})\cong{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{+}({\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C})\oplus{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{-}({\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C})
.
Two representations
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}({\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C})
are not isomorphic to each other.
Clifford module
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}({\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C})
can be defined globally in
{\mathcal{}M}
owing to the covering map, which is simply denoted by
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}({\mathcal{}M})
.

A connection form of the spinor bundle is obtained by equating it to the spin-connection form.
Moreover, a spinor is lifted to a section in
{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
owing to the coverage map.
Covariant differential
d_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
concerning
Spin(1,3)
with connection
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
, namely a Clifford connection, acts on the spinor as
[
35
,
38
,
39
]

\displaystyle d_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\psi

\displaystyle:=d\psi+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\psi,

(80)

where

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}

\displaystyle:=-i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{w}}\cdot{S}\hskip 1.00006pt=-\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta_{\circ\circ}\eta_{\star\star}{\mathfrak{w}}^{{\circ}\star}{S}^{{\circ}\star},~~\textrm{and}~~{S}:={S}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow+0}.

(81)

A coupling constant in the spinor bundle is set to the same as that of the inertial bundle.
Corresponding curvature two-form
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}

\displaystyle:=d{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}.

(82)

The Bianchi identities are provided as

\displaystyle\left(d_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)^{2}={c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{R}\cdot{S}~\mathrm{and}~~d_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=0,

(83)

where
\mathfrak{R}\cdot{{S}}=\mathfrak{R}^{\circ\circ}{{S}}_{\circ\circ}/2
using the trivial basis.
Dirac operator

{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}:\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)\rightarrow\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)

is defined as

\displaystyle{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}(\psi)

\displaystyle:=\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)\longrightarrow^{\hskip-10.65283pt{}^{d_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}}\hskip 3.00003pt\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{1}({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)\longrightarrow^{\hskip-12.2917pt{}^{\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}}}\hskip 3.99994pt\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right),

(84a)

which is expressed using the trivial basis as

\displaystyle{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}\partial_{\circ}-\frac{i}{2}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}_{\circ}^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}^{~{\star\star}}{{S}}_{\star\star}\right)\psi.

(84b)

We note that

\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d\psi=(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ})(\partial_{\circ}\psi)=\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}\partial_{\circ}\psi.

A Lorentz transformation of the Dirac operator is

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left({\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}){\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}\right)\left(S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})d_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}\right)=S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}){\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}.

For the Dirac spinor, the Dirac conjugate is defined as a map

\displaystyle\tau_{{\hskip-0.81949pt\textrm{D}c}}:\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)\rightarrow\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right):\psi\mapsto\bar{\psi}:=\psi^{\dagger}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}.

Hereafter,
\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)
and
\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}({\mathcal{}S}^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)
are simply denoted as
{\mathcal{}S}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
and
{\mathcal{}S}^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
, respectively.
Chiral operator
{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}
acts on the Dirac conjugate spinor as
\bar{\psi}^{\pm}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}=\mp\bar{\psi}^{\pm}
because

\displaystyle(\psi^{\pm})^{\dagger}=\left({\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\psi^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}=i\hskip 1.00006pt(\pm\psi^{\pm})^{\dagger}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{3}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{2}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{1}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}=\mp i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}^{\pm}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{3}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{2}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{1};

yielding

\displaystyle(\psi^{\pm})^{\dagger}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}=\left(\mp i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}^{\pm}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{3}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{2}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{1}\right)\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bar{\psi}^{\pm}=\mp\bar{\psi}^{\pm}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}.

(85)

Therefore, we obtain that
\bar{\psi}\in{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{+}
for
\psi\in{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{-}
.
Terms
i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d)\psi
and
\bar{\psi}\psi
are
SO(1,3)
invariant as follows:

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d)\psi\right)

\displaystyle=i\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{\dagger}S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}\left(S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})_{~}^{-1}\right)\left(S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d)S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}\right)S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})\psi,

\displaystyle=i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d)\psi,

where
{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d\right)=S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d)S({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}})^{-1}
is used.
Similar calculations provide the
SO(1,3)
invariance for
\bar{\psi}\psi
.

Remark 3.5
.

There exists super algebra in
End\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)=End^{+}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)\oplus End^{-}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)
.

Proof.

Suppose operator
\mathcal{O}_{0}
(
\mathcal{O}_{1}
) includes an even (odd) number of
\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
’s.
For
\psi^{\pm}\in{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\pm}
,

\displaystyle{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\psi^{\pm}=\pm\psi^{\pm},~~

\displaystyle\textrm{and}~~\left\{\begin{array}[]{rrl}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\left(\mathcal{O}_{0}\psi^{\pm}\right)=&\mathcal{O}_{0}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\psi^{\pm}=&\pm\left(\mathcal{O}_{0}\psi^{\pm}\right),\\
{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\left(\mathcal{O}_{1}\psi^{\pm}\right)=&-\mathcal{O}_{1}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\psi^{\pm}=&\mp\left(\mathcal{O}_{1}\psi^{\pm}\right)\end{array}\right.

owing to (
69
).
Thus, we obtain
\mathcal{O}_{0}\in{End}^{+}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)
and
\mathcal{O}_{1}\in{End}^{-}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)
.
Moreover, it is obvious that

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{0}\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{1}\in{End}^{+}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)~~

\displaystyle\textrm{and}~~\mathcal{O}_{0}\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{0}\in{End}^{-}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right).

Therefore, the remark is maintained.
∎

The Dirac operator
{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}
belongs to
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and flips a parity of
\psi^{\pm}
.
We note that

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\bar{\psi}\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\right)\psi&=&\left(\bar{\psi}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\right)\left({\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\right)\psi\right)&=&\bar{\psi}^{+}\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\right)\psi^{+}+\bar{\psi}^{-}\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\right)\psi^{-},\\
\bar{\psi}\psi&=&\left(\bar{\psi}{\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\right)\left({\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\psi\right)&=&\bar{\psi}^{+}\psi^{-}+\bar{\psi}^{-}\psi^{+}.\end{array}

3.2.2
Gauge bundle

This section treats a principal bundle with a compact Lie group, namely the principal gauge bundle, in the inertial manifold.
We exploit the
SU(N)
group (including
U(1)
) as a structure group, that is called the gauge group.
A scalar function (field) appearing in the gauge bundle as a section is called the Higgs field in physics.

Definition 3.6

(Principal gauge bundle)
.

A principal gauge bundle is defined as a tuple such that:

\displaystyle\left({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}^{\otimes N},\pi_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}},{\mathcal{}M},SO(1,3)\otimes{SU(N)}\right),~

\displaystyle\textrm{where}~{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}^{\otimes N}:=\overbrace{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}}^{N}

A space of complex-valued scalar fields, namely the
Higgs field
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}}=(\phi^{1},\phi^{2},\cdots,\phi^{N})^{T}
is introduced as a section
{\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}}\in{\mbox{\boldmath$\Phi$}}=\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},\Omega^{0}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}^{\otimes N})\otimes{SU(N)}\right)
belonging to the fundamental representation of the
SU(N)
symmetry.

SU(N)
group operator
{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}
acts on the section as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}:{\mbox{\boldmath$\Phi$}}\rightarrow{\mbox{\boldmath$\Phi$}}:{\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}}\mapsto{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}}\right):={\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}},

that has an
(N\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997ptN)
-matrix representation such as

\displaystyle\left[{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}}\right)\right]^{I}=\left[{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}\right]^{I}_{J}\phi^{J},

where
{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}
is a unitary matrix with det
[{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}]=1
.
Lie algebra of
SU(N)
group is

\displaystyle\left[\tau^{~}_{I},\tau^{~}_{J}\right]:=i\sum_{K}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{~}_{IJK}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{K},

where
f_{{\bullet\bullet}\bullet}
is a structure constant of
SU(N)
, and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\cdots,\tau_{N^{2}-1})\in{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(N)
.
A gauge connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}:={\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}\in\Omega^{1}\otimes{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(N)
is a Lie-algebra valued one-form object.
Covariant differential
d^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip-0.81949pt\otimes{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}}
on
p
-form object
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}})
concerning
SU(N)
and
SO(3,1)
is defined as

\displaystyle d^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip-0.81949pt\otimes{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{a}}\hskip 1.00006pt:={\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{a}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt},{\mathfrak{a}}]_{\wedge}.

(86)

Real constant
{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\in\mathbb{R}
is a coupling constant of the gauge interaction in physics.
Connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
belongs to an adjoint representation of the gauge group as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\right)

\displaystyle={\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~},

(87)

which ensures
SU(N)
covariance of the covariant differential.
At the same time,
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
is a vector in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
, which is
SO(1,3)
-transformed as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}:{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\mapsto{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt})

\displaystyle={\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}.

(88)

Corresponding gauge curvature two-form
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
is defined through a structure equation such that:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}=\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}

\displaystyle:=d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt},

\displaystyle=\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{w}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}+\frac{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}{2}f^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{J}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{K}\right)\tau^{~}_{I},

(89)

where
f^{I}_{~JK}=f^{~}_{IJK}
.
Curvature
\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
is
SO(1,3)
covariant and is
SU(N)
-transformed as an adjoint representation such that:

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{-1}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}.

The first and second Bianchi identities are

\displaystyle d_{{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip-0.81949pt\otimes{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}}}^{2}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{a}}^{a}={c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\eta_{{\circ\circ}}\mathfrak{R}^{a{\circ}}\wedge{\mathfrak{a}}^{\circ}-i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\wedge{\mathfrak{a}}^{a},~~\textrm{and}~~d^{~}_{{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip-0.81949pt\otimes{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{F}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}=0,

(90)

where
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}})
.
The gauge connection and curvature are represented using the trivial bases in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
, respectively, as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}=:{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{a}{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I},~~\textrm{and}~~\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}=\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}=:\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{ab}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\hskip-1.00006pt\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{b}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I};

(91)

and the second Bianchi identity is represented as

\displaystyle\epsilon^{a{\circ\circ}{\circ}}\left(\partial_{\circ}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\circ\circ}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{~JK}{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{\circ}\mathscr{F}^{K}_{\circ\circ}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

In this expression, tensor coefficients of the gauge curvature is provided using those of the gauge connection such that:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{I}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{b}-\partial_{b}{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{a}+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{~JK}{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{a}{\mathscr{A}}^{K}_{b}+{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{\circ}{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}_{~ab},

(92)

where
{\mathcal{T}}
is a tensor coefficient of the torsion form defined as
{\mathfrak{T}}^{\bullet}=:{\mathcal{T}}^{\bullet}_{\hskip 4.09723pt{\circ\circ}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}/2
.
When the space-time manifold is torsion-less, the gauge curvature has the same representation as it in the flat space-time.

3.2.3
Spinor-gauge bundle

The gauge group introduced in the previous section also acts on the spinor section (field), which also has the
SU(N)
symmetry.
The spinor field creates matter particles from the quantum mechanical point of view; the gauge curvature acts on them as forces among matters, and the gauge connection mediates forces between the spinor fields.
A spinor-gauge bundle is a Whitney sum of Spinor- and gauge bundles.

Definition 3.7

(Principal spinor-gauge bundle)
.

A principal spinor-gauge bundle is a tuple such as

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\otimes N},\pi_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}\oplus\pi_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}},{\mathcal{}M},Spin(1,3)\otimes{SU(N)}\right)~

\displaystyle\textrm{where}~{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\otimes N}:=\overbrace{{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}^{N}.

The total space of the gauge bundle is lifted to the spin manifold.

A connection and a curvature are provided, respectively, as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}={\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}+{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt},~~\textrm{and}~~\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}=\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}+{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt},

(93)

Corresponding Bianchi identities are obtained by direct calculations as

\displaystyle\left({d}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}\right)^{2}=-i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\cdot{{S}}\otimes\hskip 1.99997pt{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}+{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes\left(-i{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\right),~{d}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}={d}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}=0.

(94)

We introduce
N
spinors such that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}=(\psi^{1},\cdots,\psi^{N})^{T}
with the
SU(N)
symmetry.
A covariant differential on
{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}\in{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\otimes N}
in the spinor-gauge bundle is defined as

\displaystyle d_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}

\displaystyle:={\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\otimes{d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\otimes{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}.

A Dirac operator concerning the spinor-gauge bundle is provided as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}

\displaystyle:\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\otimes{N}}\otimes{SU(N)}\right)\right)\rightarrow\Gamma\left({\mathcal{}M},{\Omega}^{0}\left({\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\otimes{N}}\otimes{SU(N)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle:{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}\mapsto{\not\!d}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}:=\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}(d-i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt{{\mathfrak{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt\cdot\hskip-1.00006pt{S}})\hskip 1.99997pt{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}-{i}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}{\mathfrak{A}}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}},

\displaystyle\hskip 55.00008pt=\left(\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}\partial_{\circ}-\frac{i}{2}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}_{\circ}^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}^{~{\star\star}}{{S}}_{\star\star}\right){\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}-i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{\circ}\tau^{~}_{I}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}.

3.2.4
Hodge-dual connection and curvature

This section introduces dual connections and curvatures concerning the Hodge-dual operator in the gauge, spinor and spinor-gauge bundles.

Dual curvature in gauge bundle:

We defined dual curvature in the gauge bundle as follows:

Definition 3.8

(Hodge-dual geuge-curvature)
.

A dual curvature of the
SU(N)
gauge-curvature is defined owing to the Hodge-dual operator as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}:\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\mapsto\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}:=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}_{\circ\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ},~~

\displaystyle\textrm{where}~~\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}_{ab}:=\frac{\sqrt{{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\eta}}}}}{2}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{{\circ}\star}\eta^{{\circ}\star}\hskip 1.00006pt\epsilon_{{\star\star}{ab}}.

(95)

We note that two-group operators,
{G}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}
and
{G}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
, are homomorphic to each other:

Remark 3.9
.

A following commutative diagram is maintained:

{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}

\hat{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}

{\longrightarrow}

\hat{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{\prime}

\hskip 20.00003pt\downarrow

{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}

\hskip 20.00003pt\downarrow

{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}

\hat{\mathfrak{F}}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{\prime}}

{\longrightarrow}

\hat{\mathfrak{F}}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{\prime}}^{\prime}

{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}

Proof.

The
SO(1,3)
-transformation of a dual curvature is given as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1}={\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\epsilon$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1},

\displaystyle=\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\epsilon$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1}\right)\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1}\right)\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1}\right)\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1}\right),

\displaystyle={\mbox{\boldmath$\epsilon$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}}^{-1}\right){\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{-1}.

Therefore, it is maintained that

{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}\circ{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}\left(\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\right)={\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}\circ{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}\left(\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\right).

Similar calculations show that a dual curvature is transformed under the
SU(N)

transformation as

{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}\hskip-1.99997pt(\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}})={\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}+i\hskip 1.00006ptc^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}.

Therefore, the remark is maintained.
∎

When the dual gauge-curvature is given, the dual gauge connection is provided as a solution of the structure equation.
Though the existence of the dual connection is not trivial, we assume it in this section.

Appendix
A
discusses a necessary condition to exist the dual connection.

Dual gauge connection
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
is defined as a solution of the structure equation:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}

\displaystyle=d_{\mathfrak{w}}\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\wedge\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}},

(96)

which consists of
6N
independent first-order differential equations.
We write the dual gauge connection by means of expression (
65
),
which is compatible with the definition of the dual connection as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\right)

\displaystyle={\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}\left(d_{\mathfrak{w}}\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}+\frac{{c}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{2}f^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\wedge\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{K}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\right)=\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}.

A solution of equations (
96
) for given
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
is Lie algebra-valued one-form object such that
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}=\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}=\hat{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}\in\Omega^{1}\otimes{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(N)
.
A following remark ensures that
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
is the
SU(N)
connection in the inertial manifold
{\mathcal{}M}
.

Remark 3.10
.

One-form
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
is transformed under the structure group as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}(\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}})

\displaystyle={\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{-1}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{c}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~};

thus, it is a connection of the dual gauge bundle.

Proof.

Tensor coefficients of the dual curvature defined in (
95
) are transformed as

{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}(\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}_{ab})={\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}_{ab}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{-1}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{c}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~},

owing to its definition.
One-form object
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
is defined as a solution of equation (
96
).
Hence,
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
is a connection due to Remark
2.3
.
∎

A dual map and its adjoint maps on a
SU(N)
connection are introduced through structure equations and dual maps in the curvature as follows:

{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}

\hskip 5.0pt\left({\longleftrightarrow}^{\hskip-9.01396pt{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}}\hskip 5.50003pt\right)
