is provided as a solution of the structure equation owing to the given dual curvature
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
, and the dual-spinor current
\hat{j}(\hat{\psi})
is given as a solution of the equation of motion and interacts with the dual connection.

Figure 4
:

A diagram shows a relation between spin-gauge bundle and its dual bundle. Abbreviations “str. eq.” and “E.o.M.” mean the structure function and the equation of motion, respectively.

In our case, a topological index of the Dirac monopole is provided owing to gauge connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
given as (
118
).
A direct calculation of the first Chern class concerning
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
results indeterminate integration; thus, we exploit the indirect method here.
We note that
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
is homotopically equivalent to
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
.
When two connection are embedded in the five-dimensional smooth manifold such that:

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle:=s\hskip 1.00006pt{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}+(1-s)\hskip 1.00006pt{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}\in(\Sigma\amalg\hat{\Sigma})\otimes[0,1],

where
\Sigma\cong\hat{\Sigma}\cong{S^{2}}
.
Moreover,
\overline{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
is the gauge connection at any
s\in[0,1]
because the gauge group is abelian and the structure equation is a linear equation.
Therefore, two connections have the same Chern index as
ch(\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt})=ch(\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt})=1
.
It is true for any values of
\theta\in[0,1]
.

The stationary condition of the Yang–Mills action provides the equation of motion (
116a
); thus, solutions are SD or ASD when the gauge group is
SU(N)
, and the metric is Euclidean.
In this example, the curvature is not SD nor ASD independent from the value of T because the gauge group is
U(1)
.

5.3
Index in gauge field

The four-dimensional
Euclidean
space-time has the SD- or ASD curvature as a solution of the equation of motion obtained as the stationary point of the Yang–Mills action as shown in
Section
4.2.2
.
The instanton solution
[
46
]
, is an example of the (A)SD solution, and the instanton number is a topological invariant in the four-dimensional Euclidean space-time.
This section treats the instanton solution in a flat space-time (
{\mathfrak{w}}=0
) with a
(4\hskip-1.00006pt\times\hskip-1.00006pt4)
-matrix representation of the
\theta
-metric space.
The instanton number in the Minkowski space-time is also discussed in this section.

Coupling constant
{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
is set to unity for simplicity.
The
SU(2)
generator is extended to a four dimensional vector by adding the zeroth component such that
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}}\rightarrow{{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}}_{4}:=({\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{2}/2,-i\tau_{1},-i\tau_{2},-i\tau_{3})
, where
\tau_{i}=\sigma_{i}/2
is the
SU(2)
generator.
A gauge-group element at
\xi\in{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}
has a representation such that:

\displaystyle g(\xi):=v(\xi)\hskip 1.00006pt{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\theta}=\sum_{a=0}^{3}v^{a}(\xi)\gamma_{\theta}^{a},

that yields
\textrm{det}[g(\xi)]=\left(\eta^{~}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\xi^{\circ}\xi^{\circ}\right)^{2}
, where
v^{\bullet}(\xi)
is a position vector in
{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}_{\theta}
.
A generator of the four-dimensional
rotation
in the
\theta
-metric space is provided as
\Xi:=C_{{\circ\circ}}\gamma^{\circ}_{\theta}\gamma^{\circ}_{\theta}
, where
C_{\bullet\bullet}\in\mathbb{R}
are rotation parameters.
The group element squared is an invariant under the the four-dimensional
rotation
as

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(\xi)g^{\prime}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\Xi^{-1}g(\xi)\Xi^{-1}\Xi g(\xi)\Xi=\Xi^{-1}g(\xi)g(\xi)\Xi=g(\xi)g(\xi),

\displaystyle=\eta^{~}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\xi^{\circ}\xi^{\circ}{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}.

An instanton connection at
\xi^{a}=(t,x,y,z)
is provided as

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I=1}^{4}{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}^{I}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}[{{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}}_{4}]_{I}&0\\
0&[{{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}}_{4}]_{I}\end{array}\right)=\frac{r_{\theta}^{2}}{r_{\theta}^{2}-\lambda^{2}}g(\xi)dg^{-1}(\xi)),

where

\displaystyle g(\xi):=\frac{1}{r^{~}_{\theta}}\sum_{a=0}^{3}\xi^{a}\gamma_{\theta}^{a},

\displaystyle~~\textrm{and}~~r_{\theta}^{2}:=\eta^{~}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\xi^{\circ}\xi^{\circ}=e^{i\pi\theta}t^{2}-x^{2}-y^{2}-z^{2}.

An instanton connection is provided using a chiral representation of
\gamma^{i}_{\theta}
as

\displaystyle[{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}]_{\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}_{\theta}-\lambda^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}[{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]_{\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}&0\\
0&[\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]_{\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}\end{array}\right),

where

\displaystyle~{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}_{\theta}:=(dt_{\theta},dx,dy,dz),~~~t_{\theta}:=ie^{i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006ptt,

\displaystyle~\begin{array}[]{lcclcc}[{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]_{0}&=i\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}z&x-iy\\
x+iy&-z\end{array}\right),&[\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]_{0}&=-i\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}z&x-iy\\
x+iy&-z\end{array}\right),\\
{[{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]}_{1}&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-iy&-it_{\theta}+z\\
-it_{\theta}-z&iy\end{array}\right),&[\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]_{1}&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-iy&it_{\theta}+z\\
it_{\theta}-z&iy\end{array}\right),\\
{[{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]}_{2}&=i\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x&it_{\theta}-z\\
-it_{\theta}-z&-x\end{array}\right),&[\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]_{2}&=i\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x&-it_{\theta}-z\\
it_{\theta}-z&-x\end{array}\right),\\
{[{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]}_{3}&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-it_{\theta}&-x+iy\\
x+iy&it_{\theta}\end{array}\right),&[\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}]_{3}&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}it_{\theta}&-x+iy\\
x+iy&-it_{\theta}\end{array}\right).\end{array}

The instanton has two sets of
SU(2)
connections according to
{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}
and
\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$a$}}}_{\theta}
such that:

{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}^{I}=\left[{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}^{I}\right]_{\circ}dx^{\circ}=\left(\frac{2}{r_{\theta}^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\right)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0,&(I=0),\\
-x\hskip 1.00006ptdt_{\theta}+t_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdx+z\hskip 1.00006ptdy-y\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=1),\\
-y\hskip 1.00006ptdt_{\theta}-z\hskip 1.00006ptdx+t_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdy+x\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=2),\\
-z\hskip 1.00006ptdt_{\theta}+y\hskip 1.00006ptdx-x\hskip 1.00006ptdy+t_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=3),\end{array}\right.

and

\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}^{I}=\left[\tilde{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}^{I}\right]_{\circ}dx^{\circ}=\left(\frac{2}{r_{\theta}^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\right)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0,&(I=0),\\
x\hskip 1.00006ptdt_{\theta}-t_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdx+z\hskip 1.00006ptdy-y\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=1),\\
y\hskip 1.00006ptdt_{\theta}-z\hskip 1.00006ptdx-t_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdy+x\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=2),\\
z\hskip 1.00006ptdt_{\theta}+y\hskip 1.00006ptdx-x\hskip 1.00006ptdy-t_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=3),\end{array}\right.

The instanton connection in Eudlidean space is provided as
{\mathscr{A}}_{E}:={\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow 1}
.

Instanton curvatures,
\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{\theta}
, are induced owing to the structure equation with
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}
, respectively; Euclidean and Minkowski curvatures are provided from instanton curvature in the
\theta
-metric space such as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{E}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow 1},\hskip 13.00005pt\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{E}~=~\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow 1},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{M}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow+0},\hskip 5.0pt\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{M}~=~\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow+0}.

They yield
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{\pm}_{E}=\pm\mathfrak{F}^{\pm}_{E}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{\pm}_{M}=\pm\mathfrak{F}^{\pm}_{M}
; thus, instanton solutions in the
\theta
-metric space provide the SD and ASD solutions simultaneously for the both Euclidean and Minkowski limit.
Moreover, it is true for any
\theta\in[0,1]
that

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta})=+\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}~~\textrm{and}~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}(\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta})=-\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}.

(123)

For Eudlidean-metric space, according to a sign of the second Chern class, the SD- or ASD curvature gives a solution of the Yang–Mills equation as shown in
Remark
4.5
.
On the other hand, in the Minkowski space-time, the SD- and ASD curvature do not always provide the extremal of the Yang–Mills action.
We confirm that instanton curvature
{\mathfrak{F}}^{\pm}_{M}
in the Minkowski space-time is a solution of the Yang–Mills equation:
The Yang–Mills equation in the
\theta
-metric space has a tensor component representation as (
117
).
For the SD curvature, the equation can be written using a half of components as

\displaystyle[\textrm{Y-M eqs}]_{\theta}:=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\left(\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt1;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{01}+\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt2;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{02}+\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt3;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{03}\right){{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}}^{\hskip-1.63885pt-\frac{1}{2}}dt&=&{\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{\hat{\psi}}$}}\left(e^{i\pi\theta/2}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0}\right){{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}}^{\hskip-1.63885pt-\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 1.99997ptdt,\\
\left(-{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}}^{\hskip-1.63885pt-\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt0;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{01}-\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt3;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{02}+\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt2;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{03}\right)dx&=&{\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{\hat{\psi}}$}}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{1}{{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}}\hskip 1.99997ptdx,\\
\left(\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt3;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{01}-{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}}^{\hskip-1.63885pt-\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt0;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{02}-\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt1;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{03}\right)dy&=&{\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{\hat{\psi}}$}}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{2}{{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}}\hskip 1.99997ptdy,\\
\left(-\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt2;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{01}+\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt1;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{02}-{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}}^{\hskip-1.63885pt-\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\nabla_{\hskip-1.63885pt0;I}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{03}\right)dz&=&{\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{\hat{\psi}}$}}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{3}{{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}}\hskip 1.99997ptdz,\end{array}\right.

where

\displaystyle\nabla_{a;J}:=\left(\delta^{I}_{J}\partial_{a}+f^{I}_{~JK}[{\mathscr{A}}^{~}_{\theta}]^{K}_{a}\right)\tau^{~}_{I}.

The SD Yang–Mills equations in Euclidean and Minkowski metrics are provided as

\displaystyle[\textrm{Y-M eqs}]_{E}:=\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 1-0}[\textrm{Y-M eqs}]_{\theta},~~

\displaystyle\textrm{and}~~[\textrm{Y-M eqs}]_{M}:=\lim_{\theta\rightarrow+0}[\textrm{Y-M eqs}]_{\theta}.

Accordingly, equations have a simple relation to each other such that:

\displaystyle t\mapsto-it:[\textrm{Y-M eqs}]_{E}\mapsto[\textrm{Y-M eqs}]_{M};

thus, the connection with the Minkowski metrics is provided from the Eudlidean metric as

\displaystyle t\mapsto-it:{\mathfrak{A}}_{E}^{I}\mapsto{\mathfrak{A}}_{M}^{I}.

Therefore, the Minkowski metric’s SD connection is a solution to the Yang-Mills equation in the Minkowski space-time.
It is also true for the ASD connection.

{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
is constructed owing to the one-point compactification of
{\mathcal{}M}_{E}
as follows:
For Euclidean manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{E}
, variable transformations
r_{E}\mapsto\lambda\rho
for
0<r_{E}\leq\lambda
and
r_{E}\mapsto\lambda/\rho
for
\lambda<r_{E}<\infty
are applied; thus, two compact manifolds
D^{4}
are obtained, namely
D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}
and
D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}
, respectively.
Then, surfaces of
D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}
and
D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}
are equated to each other with keeping three angular variables; thus,
{\mathcal{}M}_{E}
is compacitified into union of two compact manifolds such that
{\mathcal{}M}_{E}\mapsto{\mathcal{}M}_{1}=D_{\textrm{in}}^{4}\cup D_{\textrm{out}}^{4}
with
D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}\cap D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}=\partial{D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}}=-\partial{D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}}=S^{3}
and
\partial(D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}{\cup}D^{4}_{\textrm{out}})=\emptyset
.
The first Pontrjagin number is obtained as an integration of the Pontrjagin class in compactified volumes such that:

\displaystyle p_{1}({\mathcal{}M}_{1})=-\left(\int_{D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}}\left.\frac{{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{1}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{1}\right]}{8\pi^{2}}\right|_{r_{E}\rightarrow\lambda\rho}+\int_{D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}}\left.\frac{{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{1}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{1}\right]}{8\pi^{2}}\right|_{r_{E}\rightarrow\lambda/\rho}\right),

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{1}=\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{E}

\displaystyle=:\left.\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathscr{F}_{E}^{~}\right]_{{\circ\circ}}{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}\right|_{\theta\rightarrow 1}.

A trace of an instanton curvature squared is obtained such that:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{1}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{I=1}^{4}\epsilon_{E}^{{\circ\circ}{\circ\circ}}\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathscr{F}_{E}^{I}\right]_{{\circ\circ}}\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathscr{F}_{E}^{I}\right]_{{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{2\hskip-0.81949pt\times\hskip-0.81949pt2}\left[\left[\textbf{t}_{4}\right]_{I}.\left[\textbf{t}_{4}\right]_{I}\right]\mathfrak{v}_{\theta},

(124)

\displaystyle=-48\left(\frac{\lambda}{r_{E}^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\right)^{4}\mathfrak{v}_{\theta},

thus,
p_{1}({\mathcal{}M}_{1})
is calculated as

\displaystyle p_{1}({\mathcal{}M}_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\rho^{3}}{(\rho^{2}+1)^{4}}d\rho\hskip 1.99997pt\int_{S^{3}}d\Omega_{4}~=~1,

where
d\Omega_{4}
is an integration measure for an angular integration on
S^{3}
, which gives
\int_{S^{3}}d\Omega_{4}=2\pi^{2}
.
The completely anti-symmetric tensor in Eudlidean space is provided as
[{\mbox{\boldmath$\epsilon$}}_{E}]^{0123}=[{\mbox{\boldmath$\epsilon$}}_{E}]_{0123}=1
.

On intersection
D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}\cap D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}=S^{3}
, angular variables coincide as

\displaystyle\partial D_{\textrm{in}}\ni\theta_{i}^{\textrm{in}}=\theta_{i}^{\textrm{out}}\in-\partial D_{\textrm{out}},

for
i=1,2,3
with keeping an orientation on their surfaces.
When
\theta_{3}^{\textrm{in}}
maps to
\theta_{3}^{\textrm{out}}
as
n\times\hskip 1.99997pt\theta_{3}^{\textrm{in}}=\theta_{3}^{\textrm{out}}
with
0<n\in\mathbb{Z}
, an instanton number is given as
p_{1}({\mathcal{}M}_{1})=n
due to the
\theta_{3}
integration from
0
to
2n\pi
, which corresponds to the winding number around infinity.

The first Pontrjagin number is provided in the entire space of
W_{\theta}:={{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}}\otimes(\theta\in[0,1])
.
After the same compactification as in a Euclidean space in
W_{\theta}
, a four-dimensional polar coordinate is introduced as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\hskip 1.99997ptt:=\lambda\rho\cos{\theta_{1}},&x:=\lambda\rho\sin{\theta_{1}}\cos{\theta_{2}},\\
y:=\lambda\rho\sin{\theta_{1}}\sin{\theta_{2}}\cos{\theta_{3}},&z:=\lambda\rho\sin{\theta_{1}}\sin{\theta_{2}}\sin{\theta_{3}}.\end{array}

The first Pontrjagin number is provided by integrations such that:

\displaystyle p_{1}({\mathcal{}M}_{\theta})

\displaystyle=-i\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}e^{i\pi\theta/2}\int_{0}^{1}d\rho\int_{0}^{\pi}\rho^{2}\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\theta_{1}\int_{0}^{\pi}\rho\sin{\theta_{2}}\hskip 1.99997ptd\theta_{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta_{3}

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\left[\left\{1+\rho^{2}\left(1-\left(1+e^{i\pi\theta}\right)\cos^{2}{\theta_{1}}\right)\right\}^{-4}+\left\{\left(1+e^{i\pi\theta}\right)\cos^{2}{\theta_{1}}-\left(1+\rho^{2}\right)\right\}^{-4}\right],

\displaystyle=1,

for
0<\theta\leq 1
.
We note that
\epsilon_{E}^{{\circ\circ}{\circ\circ}}
in (
124
) is replaced by
{{\sigma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\epsilon_{\theta}^{{\circ\circ}{\circ\circ}}
in the
\theta
-metric space.
The Pontrjagin class with the Minkowski metric is a divergent integral; thus, it is not well-defined.
A domain of the instanton solution in the inertial bundle with the Minkowski metric is not the entire space of
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
but is
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}\hskip-3.00003pt\setminus\hskip-3.00003pt{\mathcal{}M}_{\lambda}
, where
{\mathcal{}M}_{\lambda}:=\{\xi\in{\mathcal{}M}_{0}\mid{\eta_{\circ\circ}\xi^{\circ}\xi^{\circ}-\lambda^{2}=0}\}
; thus, manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
is not closed as a principal bundle with the instanton connection.
Taking the closure of manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
with respect to
\theta
from the right (
\theta=+0
),
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
has the first Pontrjagin number as
p_{1}({\mathcal{}M}_{\theta})|_{\theta\rightarrow+0}=1
.
The instanton solution in the Minkowski metric is an example of
Theorem
2.16
: manifolds
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
are cobordant to each other, and the first Pontrjagin number of
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}
is consistently defined owing to that of
{\mathcal{}M}_{1}
.

The SD- and ASD-instanton-curvatures fulfil the Bianchi identity as

\displaystyle d\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm}+\left[\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm},{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}^{\pm}\right]_{\wedge}=0.

When we take the SD- or ASD solution as the principal curvature of the gauge bundle, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}=\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm}=\pm{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm})=\pm\hat{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~};

thus, the equation of motion is provided from (
108b
) as

\displaystyle{d}\hat{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}+\left[\hat{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~},{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}\right]_{\wedge}=\pm{d}\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}\pm\left[\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~},{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}\right]_{\wedge}=\pm\bar{\psi}_{\textrm{inst}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}\psi_{\textrm{inst}}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{V}_{\circ}=0,

where
\psi_{\textrm{inst}}
is an instanton spinor-field.
Last equality is due to the Bianchi identity.
This is true for any
\theta\in(0,1]
.
Therefore, the source field does not exist in a domain of the instanton curvature.

Figure 5:

This figure shows regions where
\|\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\|>1
for the Eudlidean metric (a circle at the origin) and for the Minkowski metric (a mashed region along diagonal lines).
Here, we set
\lambda=1
in
\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
.

A physical interpretation of an instanton in the Minkowski-bundle is as follows:
The SD- and ASD-connections domain with the Minkowski metric is
{\mathcal{}M}_{0}\hskip-3.00003pt\setminus\hskip-3.00003pt{\mathcal{}M}_{\lambda}
; thus the domain of
\psi_{\textrm{inst}}
is
{\mathcal{}M}_{\lambda}
, which is on the light-cone at far from the origin, as shown in Figure
5
.
After quantization of spinor field
\psi_{\textrm{inst}}
, a quantity

\displaystyle p_{\textrm{inst}}\left(\Sigma_{3}\right):=\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\bar{\psi}_{\textrm{inst}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\circ}\psi_{\textrm{inst}}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{V}_{\circ}

may give a provability to observe an instanton in three-dimensional volume
\Sigma_{3}\in{\mathcal{}M}_{\lambda}
after appropriate normalization; thus, an instanton is localized in the light-cone and is interpreted as a massless particle with an
SU(2)
-charge.
Conservation of the Minkowski-instanton number is ensured as the topological invariant (the first Pontrjagin number) owing to cobordism.

5.4
Index in chiral representation

This section discusses the topological index of the Yang–Mills bundle using a new
2N
-dimensional vector representation, namely a chiral representation.
A spinor-gauge bundle has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading total space as
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\otimes N}=({{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{+}})^{\otimes N}\oplus({{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{-}})^{\otimes N}
owing to projection operator
P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
as discussed in section
3.2.1
.
The chiral representation of spinors and covariant differential operator are introduced as

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}_{ch}

\displaystyle:=\gamma_{\textrm{Y}\hskip-0.81949pt\textrm{M}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\not\!d}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}&0\\
0&P^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&{\not\!d^{-}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\\
{\not\!d^{+}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}&0\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}}_{ch}

\displaystyle:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}^{+}\\
{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}^{-}\end{array}\right),

where
{\not\!d^{\pm}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}}:={\not\!d^{~}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}}{{\circ}}P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
.
A Dirac conjugate of
{\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}}_{ch}
is provided as

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\overline{\Psi}$}}_{ch}:={\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}}^{\dagger}_{ch}\gamma^{0}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{{\textrm{Y}\hskip-0.81949pt\textrm{M}}}=\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{\hat{\psi}}$}}^{-},{\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{\hat{\psi}}$}}^{+}\right).

In this representation, the Lagrangian of the spinor field is provided as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{L}}_{ch}:=-\left(\hskip 1.00006pt\overline{{\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}}}_{ch}\hskip 1.99997pti{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}_{ch}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}}_{ch}+\frac{\mu}{\hbar}\hskip 1.99997pt\overline{{\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}}}_{ch}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}}_{ch}\right)\mathfrak{v},

which provides the same equation of motion as those from the matter-field Lagrangian in the Yang–Mills bundle.

A kinetic term of the matter Lagrangian denoted by
K_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}:=i\bar{\psi}\hskip 1.00006pt{\not\!d}\psi
is Hermitian self-conjugate as follows:
For the Dirac operator given in (
84a
), its Hermitian conjugate is provided that

\displaystyle K_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d)\psi\right)^{\dagger}=-i\hskip 1.00006pt\left(\partial_{\star}\psi^{\dagger}\right)(\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\star})^{\dagger}(\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{0})^{\dagger}\psi=-i\hskip 1.00006pt\left(\partial_{\star}\bar{\psi}\right)\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\star}\psi,

(125)

\displaystyle=i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\star}(\partial_{\star}\psi)-i\hskip 1.00006pt\left(\partial_{\star}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\star}\psi\right),

and the last term is vanished owing to the current conservation; thus,
K_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}
is a Hermitian self-conjugate as
K_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}=K_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}^{\dagger}
.
Moreover,
\mathrm{Ker}({\not\!d^{-}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}})^{\dagger}\cong\mathrm{Coker}({\not\!d^{+}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}})={\mathcal{}S}^{-}/\mathrm{Im}({\not\!\mathcal{}D}^{+})
is provided owing to
(\ref{Ksp})
and

\displaystyle i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}^{+}(\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d)\psi^{-}=-i\hskip 1.00006pt\left(\partial_{\star}\bar{\psi}^{+}\right)\gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{\star}\psi^{-}=-i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\psi}^{+}(\overleftarrow{\iota_{{\gamma}_{\hskip-0.73616pts\hskip-0.73616ptp\hskip 0.73616pt}}d})\psi^{-},

where
(\overleftarrow{\bullet})
is a differential operator acting from the left.
Operator
{\not\!d^{~}_{{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}}
is the Dirac operator; thus the Atyah–Singer index theorem (
Remark
2.12
) is maintained in the spinor-gauge bundle after appropriate compactification of base manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{E}
.

The topological index with the Minkowski metric is defined owing to cobordism through the
\theta
-metric space.
The Dirac equation is provided in the
\theta
-metric space as

\displaystyle{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}

\displaystyle:=\iota_{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\theta}}(d-i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt{{\mathfrak{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt\cdot\hskip-1.00006pt{S}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}){\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}}\otimes{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}-{i}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt\iota_{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{\theta}}{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mbox{\boldmath$\psi$}},

where

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt\cdot\hskip-1.00006pt{S}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}

\displaystyle:=\frac{i}{2}\eta^{~}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\eta^{~}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\star\star}}{\mathcal{E}}^{\mu}_{a}\omega^{~{\circ}\star}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{{S}}^{{\circ}\star}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}.

Spinor bundle is formally extended to the
\theta
-metric space as
\left({\mathcal{}S}^{\theta}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},\pi^{\theta}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta},Spin_{\theta}\right)
, where
{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}
is a Riemannian manifold with metric tensor
{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}^{~}_{\theta}
defined in (
16a
) and
{\mathcal{}S}^{\theta}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is a Clifford module with the Clifford algebra
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{~}_{\theta}
defined in (
75
).
At
\theta\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt+0
and
\theta\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt1
, the
\theta
-metric gives Minkowski and Euclid spinor bundles with
Spin_{\theta}|_{\theta\rightarrow+0}=Spin(1,3)
and
Spin_{\theta}|_{\theta\rightarrow 1}=Spin(4)
, respectively.
Dirac spinor
\psi^{~}_{\theta}
is presented as a section in the
\theta
-metric spinor bundle; thus, the chiral representation introduced above is extended in the
\theta
-metric spinor bundle.

First, the topological index of Eudlidean space-time is considered in the
\theta
metric space at
\theta=1
.
Dirac operator
{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}^{E}_{ch}:={\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}^{\theta}_{ch}|_{\theta\rightarrow 1}
has the topological index introduced in section
2.5.1
, where
{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}^{\theta}_{ch}
is a Dirac operator in the
\theta
-metric extended chiral space.
Euclidean base-manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{E}
is assumed to be compactified appropriately.
Owing to the McKean–Singer theorem (given as
Remark
2.11
in section
2.5.1
), the index of Dirac operator
{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}_{ch}^{E}
is provided using the super trace such that:

\displaystyle ind\left(i{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}_{ch}^{E}\right)

\displaystyle=Str\left(e^{-t\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}}\right)={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949ptE}\hskip 1.99997pte^{-t\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}}\right],

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[e^{-t\hskip 1.63885pt{{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}}^{\hskip-0.73616pt-}\hskip 1.63885pt{{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}}^{\hskip-0.73616pt+}}\right]-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[e^{-t\hskip 1.63885pt{{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}}^{\hskip-0.73616pt+}\hskip 1.63885pt{{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}}^{\hskip-0.73616pt-}}\right],

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{}M}_{E}}\hat{A}(\mathfrak{R})ch(\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}).

where
{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}^{E}
is the Dirac operator in the spinor-gauge bundle in Eudlidean space-time and
{{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}^{E}}^{\pm}
are its projections owing to the projection operators
P^{\pm}_{E}
, that is introduced in section
2.6
.
Operator
{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}^{E}\hskip 1.99997pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}^{E}
is an elliptic type; thus,
e^{-t\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}^{E}}
is a heat kernel.
The last equality is owing to the Atiyah–Singer index theorem given as
Remark
2.12
in section
2.5.1
.

Second, we discuss the topological index with the Minkowski metric, which is not well-defined because operator
{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}\hskip 1.99997pt{\not\!\mathcal{}D}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}
is a hyperbolic type, and the Atiyah–Singer index theorem is not fulfilled.
Although the index theorem is not applied as its original shape, the topological index is defined in the Minkowski space-time by
Definition
2.16
.
Minkowski manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{M}:=\left.{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}\right|_{\theta\rightarrow+0}
and Euclidean manifold
{\mathcal{}M}_{E}:=\left.{\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}\right|_{\theta\rightarrow 1}
are cobordant to each other with respect to manifold
W_{\theta}:={\mathcal{}M}_{\theta}\otimes(\theta\in[0,1])
.
Therefore, the topological index in
{\mathcal{}M}_{M}
is well-defined as

\displaystyle ind\left(i{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right):=ind\left(i{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}_{ch}^{E}\right)=\int_{{\mathcal{}M}_{E}}\hat{A}(\mathfrak{R})ch(\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt})\Rightarrow\int_{{\mathcal{}M}_{E}}ch(\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}).

We note that the second Chern class is zero for know solutions of the Einstein equation; thus, the index is given only by curvature
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
.
The index in the Minkowski space-time is formally denoted as

\displaystyle ind\left(i{\mbox{\boldmath${\not\!\mathcal{}D}$}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pte^{-t\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}\hskip 1.63885pt{{\not\!\mathcal{}D}}_{\hskip-1.47221pts\hskip-0.73616ptg}}\right],

where
\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=\gamma^{5}
in the standard notation, and it is equivalent to the standard representation of the axial anomaly
[
13
,
24
]
.

Table 1:

Principal bundles introduced in this study.
In a bundle name, “SG” stands for “spinor-gauge”.

This study treated the classical Yang–Mills theory and general relativity in four-dimension.
The Chern–Weil theory concerning several principal bundles twisting to each other provides a common mathematical language to treat physical objects appearing in the Yang–Mills theory in curved space-time.
In reality, we introduced five principal bundles listed in
Table
1
.
The co-Poincaré bundle is a model of our Universe, which provides the platform of the Yang–Mills theory.
Space-time manifold
\mathscr{M}
with, in general, non-zero curvature is a smooth Riemannian four-dimensional manifold.
At each point of
\mathscr{M}
, inertial manifold
{\mathcal{}M}
with vanishing Levi-Civita connection
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{~\mu\nu}
is associated as the inertial space owing to Einstein’s equivalent principle.
inertial manifold
{\mathcal{}M}
has vierbain form
{\mathfrak{e}}
as a section that determines a metric of
{\mathcal{}M}
and connection
{\mathfrak{w}}
to govern gravitational interaction among any energetic objects.
Other bundles provide matter and force fields on the base manifold
{\mathcal{}M}
as sections and connections, respectively, and are twisted with the co-Poincaré bundle.

The Yang–Mills theory is represented in the spinor-gauge bundles on the inertial manifold with various structure groups of the gauge symmetry.
Connection and curvature of the bundle belonging to the fundamental representation of the structure group represent a potential function and a force field in physics, e.g.,
U(1)
-gauge group as the electromagnetic force,
SU(2)
as the weak force, and
SU(3)
as the strong force, in physics.
The matter field is a spinor-valued section in total space
{\mathcal{}S}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
of the spinor-gauge bundle and interacts with each other through the gauge connection.
A scalar field in the gauge bundle with the structure group of
SU(2)
is called the Higgs field in physics, which is a section of the
SU(2)
-gauge bundle and belongs to the fundamental representation of the
SU(2)
symmetry.
The equation of motion for the spinor fields consists of the Dirac operator; thus, the Atiyah–Singer index theorem provides the topological invariant in the spinor-gauge bundle.

The Hodge-dual of curvature forms are indispensable objects to constructing Yang-Mills theory; a force field given by curvature has a constraint owing to the Bianchi identity
d^{~}_{G}\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{G}=0
; thus, a gauge curvature
\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{G}
does not have dynamics in the spinor-gauge bundle.
On the other hand, the dual curvature is not a curvature in the spinor-gauge bundle and has an equation of motion
d^{~}_{G}\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{G}=j^{\circ}\mathfrak{V}_{\circ}
.
Therefore, the dual-curvature has dynamics in the spinor-gauge bundle.
In four-dimensional spaces, the Hodge-dual of a two-form object is again a two-form object; thus, the curvature two-form is decomposed into the SD and ASD parts.
A solution to the equation of motion in Euclidean space is a pure SD- or ASD curvature; thus, the equation of motion is equivalent to the Bianchi identity. Therefore, the gauge field cannot have dynamics in the gauge bundle.

When a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading discrete operator, such as the Hodge-dual operator, exists for the principal curvature, duplex superspace appears in the system.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading operator splits a space of curvatures into the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading superspaces
V^{\pm}(\Omega^{2})
, namely the primary superspace, owing to eigenvalues concerning the operator.
Owing to the isomorphism of four-dimensional spaces (
2.3
), a space of one-form objects also splits into two subspaces; SD- and ASD curvatures are obtained using one-form objects in one of these subspaces.
A pair of the principal curvature and its dual curvature provides the Kramers pair.
The secondary
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading superspace arises using the one-dimensional Clifford algebra in the
({2\hskip-1.00006pt\times\hskip-1.00006pt2})
-matrix representation of the Kramers pair.
The Dirac operator is provided in the secondary superspace, and the Atiyah–Singer index theorem provides the additional topological invariants in the principal bundles.
General relativity and Yang–Mills Lagrangians are represented using supertrace in the secondary superspace.
The similarity between general relativity and Yang–Mills gauge fields, in other words, similarity among four forces in Nature, is evident in our representation.

A procedure to extract topological invariants in the secondary superspace of the principal bundles commonly also appears in material physics.
The topological insulator’s edge-bulk correspondence, such as the charge- and spin-pump phenomena, is an example appearing as a topological invariant in the secondary superspace
[
47
,
48
,
49
]
.
In this case, the time-reversal operator plays the role of the Hodge-dual operator in this study. The electron wave function and its time-reversal provide the Kramers pair of the fermionic section.
The principal bundle consists of the Brillouin zone as the base manifold, and the vector space of the electron wave functions as the total space; it has the
U(2)
symmetry as the structure group and the Berry connection as the principal connection.
The spin polarization is the topological invariant expressed by the first Chern number.

General relativity and the Yang–Mills theory are fundamental theories of cosmology and elementary particle physics and treat fundamental phenomena in our universe; thus, they are defined in the four-dimensional manifold with the Minkowski metric and the Bianchi identity and an equation of motion are the hyperbolic type.
On the other hand, the Atiyah–Singer index theorem holds for the elliptic-type Dirac operator on a compact manifold.
Therefore, we cannot apply the theorem on general relativity and the Yang–Mills theory as its original shape, even after appropriately compacting the space-time.
We introduced the
\theta
-metric, which smoothly connects the Minkowski space-time to Euclidean space-time.
When a compact manifold with the Minkowski metric is isotopic to that with the Eudlidean metric, two manifolds are cobordant, and characteristic numbers are cobordism invariant.
In this case, a characteristic index in Minkowski space-time is defined owing to the corresponding index in Euclidean space-time, and the index theorem ensures the index is topological invariant.
We provided several examples of topological invariants defined in the
\theta
-metric space and demonstrated topological invariants working in the Minkowski space-time and Euclidean space-time.
Hayashi utilised a similar method to prove the bulk-edge correspondence for two-dimensional type
A
and type
AII
topological insulators owing to the cobordism invariance of the index
[
50
]
.
The method using the
\theta
-metric may have various applications on the wide variety of physical topics.

A summary table of the Lagrangians appearing in the Yang–Mills theory.

Appendix A
existence of dual connection and dual spinor

A.1
U(1)
gauge group

First, this appendix treats the most straightforward case that the
U(1)
group in the flat space-time (
{\mathfrak{w}}=0
).
In this case, the dual connection is obtained as a solution of structure equation
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}=d\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
, that satisfies the Bianchi identity
d\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}=d(d\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}})=0
.
The existence problem of a solution for this system is equivalent to the differential-geometrical question: “Is closed-form
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
exact ?”.
For a contractible manifold, the answer to this question is positively given by the Poincaré lemma; the general answer is provided using the de Rham theorem (See, e.g.,
[
51
]
).
The de Rham theorem asserts that
k
’th de Rham cohomology of a smooth manifold
M
,
H_{{d\hskip-0.81949ptR}}^{k}(M)
, is isomorphic to the real cohomology
H^{k}(M,\mathbb{R})
.

The existence problem of the one-form object
{\mathfrak{a}}
such that
d{\mathfrak{a}}={\mathfrak{b}}
for given two-form object
{\mathfrak{b}}
is answered by a following statement
[
51
]
:

Remark A.1
.

Suppose
M
is
n
-dimensional smooth manifold and closed
k
-form object
{\mathfrak{b}}
exists in a compact submanifold of
M
;
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{b}}\subseteq M
.
In this case, following two statements are equivalent to each other:

1.

(k-1)
-form object
{\mathfrak{a}}
such that
{\mathfrak{b}}=d{\mathfrak{a}}
exists in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathfrak{b}}
, where
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}
is a compact and connected manifold.

2.

The
(n-k)
’th relative homology of pair
(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}},\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
is vanished;

H_{n-k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}},\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}};\mathbb{R})=0
, where
\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}\subset\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}
is a boundary of compact and connected manifold
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}
.

Proof.

1.

\Rightarrow

2.
:
Closedness of two-form
{\mathfrak{b}}
is consistent with an exactness
{\mathfrak{b}}=d{\mathfrak{a}}
such that
d{\mathfrak{b}}=dd{\mathfrak{a}}=0
.
The
k
’th de Rham cohomology of domain
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}
concerning the external-derivative operation is defined as

\displaystyle H^{k}_{{d\hskip-0.81949ptR}}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})

\displaystyle:=Z^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})/B^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})=0,

where

\displaystyle Z^{k}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Ker}\left(d:\Omega^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})\rightarrow\Omega^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})\right),

\displaystyle B^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Im}\left(d:\Omega^{k-1}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})\rightarrow\Omega^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})\right).

Owing to the de Rham theorem and the Poincaré duality-theorem for a compact and connected four-dimensional manifold, there exists isomorphism:

H^{k}_{{d\hskip-0.81949ptR}}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})\rightarrow H^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}};\mathbb{R})\rightarrow H_{n-k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}},\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}};\mathbb{R}).

Thus, vanishing relative homology
H_{n-k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}},\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}};\mathbb{R})=0
is maintained.

2.

\Rightarrow

1.
:
When
H_{n-k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}},\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}};\mathbb{R})=0
, the
k
’th de Rham cohomology is also vanished owing to the de Rham theorem and the Poincaré duality theorem.
The vanishing
k
’th cohomology induces isomorphism between
Z^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
and
B^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
.
If
B^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
is empty,
Z^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
is also empty, which contradicts to the assumption of the existence of
{\mathfrak{b}}\in Z^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
.
Therefore, the existence of
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{k-1}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
such that
B^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})\cong[d{\mathfrak{a}}]\equiv[{\mathfrak{b}}]{\cong}Z^{k}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{a}})
is maintained.
∎

For an abelian gauge symmetry such as a
U(1)
gauge group, the structure equation is equivalent to the closedness of the curvature; thus, the existence of the dual connection is stated as the following theorem:

Theorem A.2
.

Suppose Lie algebra valued two-form object
\mathfrak{F}
with an abelian gauge group in the flat space-time is given in submanifold
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq{\mathcal{}M}
.
Following two statements are equivalent to each other:

1.

Lie-algebra valued one-form object
{\mathfrak{A}}
exists in compact manifold
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathfrak{F}}
as a solution of structure equation
\mathfrak{F}=d{\mathfrak{A}}
, and the principal bundle is constructed using connection
{\mathfrak{A}}
and curvature
\mathfrak{F}
.

2.

The second homology is vanished in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}}
;
H_{2}(\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{A}}},\partial\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{A}}};\mathbb{R})=0
.

Proof.

Vanishing second homology is equivalent to existence of connection
{\mathfrak{A}}
owing to
Remark
A.1
.
When connection
{\mathfrak{A}}
is provided as a solution of structure equation
\mathfrak{F}=d{\mathfrak{A}}
from the given
\mathfrak{F}
, it is Lie algebra valued one-form belonging to an adjoint representation as shown in
Remark
2.2
.
∎

Example A.3
.

Coulomb-type potential

A domain of a connection for thr Coulomb-type potential is
\Sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}:={T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\{r=0\}
, as given in
Example
5.1
, and corresponding curvature
(\ref{FU1})
has the same domain.
For dual curvature
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
given by (
120
), tensor coefficient
\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
is also defined in
{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
except
r=0
and is static (no time dependence); thus, the domain of
\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
is
\Sigma_{\hat{\mathscr{F}}}={T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\{r=0\}\cong\mathbb{R}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{3}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\{r=0\})
.
The second homology of a compact submanifold of the domain;
\overline{\Sigma}_{\hat{\mathscr{F}}}:=D^{1}{\otimes}(D^{3}{{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}}\{0\})
is calculated as
H_{2}(\overline{\Sigma}_{\hat{\mathscr{F}}};\mathbb{R})\cong H_{2}(D^{1},S^{0};\mathbb{R}){\oplus}H_{2}(D^{3},S^{2}{\oplus}S^{2};\mathbb{R})=0
; thus, there exists dual connection
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
in
\Sigma_{\hat{\mathscr{A}}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\hat{\mathscr{F}}}
.

Example A.4
.

Linear electric current

A linear electric current makes a stationary magnetic field around the current in a flat space-time manifold, and it has a
U(1)
connection such that:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)}\hskip 1.00006ptdz\in\Omega^{1}\left({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\{x=0\wedge y=0\}\right).

Corresponding curvature and dual curvature are respectively provided as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle=\frac{x}{x^{2}+y^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptdx\wedge dz+\frac{y}{x^{2}+y^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptdy\wedge dz

\displaystyle\overset{{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}}{\longrightarrow}\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle=i\frac{y}{x^{2}+y^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptdt{\wedge}dx-i\frac{x}{x^{2}+y^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptdt{\wedge}dy,

The dual connection is provided as a solution of structure equation (
96
) such that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle=i\tan^{-1}{\frac{y}{x}}\hskip 1.99997ptdt,

which is indeterminate at
x\hskip-1.00006pt=\hskip-1.00006pty\hskip-1.00006pt=\hskip-1.00006pt0
with any
z
; thus, its domain is
\Sigma_{\hat{\mathscr{A}}}\cong\mathbb{R}\otimes\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\mathbb{R}\right)
.
The second homology of
\Sigma_{\hat{\mathscr{A}}}
is the same as that of
Example
5.1
such that
H_{2}(\overline{\Sigma}_{\hat{\mathscr{A}}};\mathbb{R}){\cong}H_{2}(D^{1};\mathbb{R}){\oplus}H_{2}(D^{1}{\otimes}S^{1},T^{2};\mathbb{R})=0
; thus, the dual connection exists in the same domain.
A domain of the dual connection is the same as that of the dual curvature.

The second Chern class for
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
is provided as

c^{~}_{2}({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt})=c^{~}_{2}(\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt})=\left((4\pi(x^{2}+y^{2})\right)^{-2}.

Example A.5
.

Plane wave

A plane wave solution in globally flat
\mathscr{M}
is considered.
A
U(1)
connection and corresponding curvature are, respectively, provided using the Cartesian coordinate system as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle=\sin{(t-z)}\left(dx+dy\right),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle=\cos{(t-z)}\left(dt\wedge dx+dt\wedge dy+dx\wedge dz+dy\wedge dz\right),

which represent plane wave lying in a
(x,y)
-plane propagating to
z
direction with speed
c=1
, and its dual curvature is obtained as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}=\cos{(t-z)}\left(dt\wedge dx-dt\wedge dy+dx\wedge dz-dy\wedge dz\right).

The domain of
\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}
is the entire space of
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}{\cong}\mathbb{R}^{4}
, which provides the vanishing second homology;
H_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\not\!0;\mathbb{R})=0
.
The dual connection is provided as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt(1)\hskip-0.81949pt}=i\sin{(t-z)}\left(dx-dy\right),

whose domain is also entire
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
.

A.2
SU(N)
gauge group

Next, we treat a non-abelian gauge with
SU(N)
group in the flat space-time.
In this case, exactness and closedness are corresponding to the structure equation and Bianchi identity, respectively, such that:

\displaystyle\emph{Covariant-exactness}\hskip 3.00003pt:

\displaystyle\hskip 11.00008pt\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 3.99994pt=d\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\wedge\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}},

(
96
)
’

\displaystyle\emph{Covariant-closedness}:

\displaystyle\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{d}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}=d\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt[\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}},\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}]_{\wedge}=0,

(
97
)
’

where
SO(1,3)
-covariant differential
d_{\mathfrak{w}}
in (
96
) and in (
97
) is replaced by the external derivative owing to the flat space-time.
As is the case of an abelian gauge, when the dual curvature is
covariant-exact
(given by the structure equation
()’
‣
A.2
, it is trivially
covariant-closed
owing to the Bianchi identity
()’
‣
A.2
.
The question here is that “Does dual curvature
\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
satisfying
()’
‣
A.2
have a solution for
()’
‣
A.2
?”.
A key to treat this question is the Chern–Simons form, which is introduced in the spinor-gauge bundle as follows:

Definition A.6

(Chern–Simons form)
.

The Chern–Simons forms in a gauge bundle is defined as a functional of
\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{\#}
in the total space such that:

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}({\mathfrak{A}}^{\#}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}):={{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}^{I}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}({\mathfrak{A}}^{\#}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt})\hskip 1.99997ptt_{I}:=-i\left[{\mathfrak{A}}^{\#}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt},d{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{\#}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{\#}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{\#}\right]_{\wedge}.

(126)

A superscript “
#
” represents a pull-buck of forms from the base manifold to the total manifold with respect to the bundle map.

Hereafter, “
#
” and a subscript “
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}
” are omitted for simplicity unless otherwise denoted.
The Chern–Simons form of a dual connection is defined by
(\ref{CSform})
with replacing
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
to
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
.
Discussions below are common for both principal- and dual connections.

We introduce a map

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{a}}:\Omega^{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{p-1}:\left[{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}\right]_{\wedge}\mapsto i{\mathfrak{b}},

for
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{1}
and
{\mathfrak{b}}\in\Omega^{p-1}
.
An anti-derivative operator
\delta_{\mathfrak{a}}:\Omega^{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{p-1}
is defined in the total space of the principal bundle as follows:

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{W}}({\mathfrak{A}})

\displaystyle=\pi^{~}_{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{{\mathfrak{W}}\left({\mathfrak{A}}+\varepsilon{\mathfrak{a}}\right)-{\mathfrak{W}}\left({\mathfrak{A}}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right),

(127)

where
{\mathfrak{W}}({\mathfrak{A}})
is an invariant polynomial in Weil algebra.
de Rham cohomology of Weil algebra with respect to anti-derivative operator
\delta_{\mathfrak{a}}
is trivial such that:

\displaystyle H_{{d\hskip-0.81949ptR}}^{k}(W({\mathfrak{A}});\delta_{\mathfrak{a}})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}&(k=0)\\
0&(k>0),\end{cases}

(128)

where
W({\mathfrak{A}})\in{\mathfrak{W}}({\mathfrak{A}})
is Weil algebra appearing in the gauge bundle.

Remark A.7
.

The principal curvature form in the total space is an exact form by means of the Chern–Weil theory, and there exists the Chern–Simons form which gives the curvature form such that
\mathfrak{F}=\delta_{\mathfrak{a}}{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}
.

Proof.

Suppose the curvature is provided as a solution of the structure equation.
The Chern–Simons form is expressed using
{\mathfrak{A}}
as

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}^{I}\hskip-3.00003pt\left({\mathfrak{A}}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}\wedge\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}^{K}+\frac{{c}}{2}f^{K}_{~LM}{\mathfrak{A}}^{L}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{M}\right),

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{f^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.99997ptd\hskip-1.00006pt\left({\mathfrak{A}}^{J}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{K}\right)+{c}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{K}_{~LM}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{L}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{M}\right\}.

Anti-derivative operator is applied on the Chern–Simons form as follows:

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathfrak{a}}{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=\pi^{~}_{\mathfrak{a}}\left(f^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{a}}^{J}\hskip-1.99997pt\wedge\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}^{K}+\frac{{c}}{2}f^{K}_{~LM}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{L}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{M}\right)t_{I}\right)=-i\pi^{~}_{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\left[{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{F}\right]_{\wedge}\right)=\mathfrak{F},

where the Jacobi identity
f_{\hskip-0.81949ptA\hskip-0.81949ptB\hskip-0.81949ptE}\hskip 3.00003ptf_{\hskip-0.81949ptE\hskip-0.81949ptC\hskip-0.81949ptD}+f_{\hskip-0.81949ptC\hskip-0.81949ptB\hskip-0.81949ptE}\hskip 3.00003ptf_{\hskip-0.81949ptA\hskip-0.81949ptE\hskip-0.81949ptD}+f_{\hskip-0.81949ptD\hskip-0.81949ptB\hskip-0.81949ptE}\hskip 3.00003ptf_{\hskip-0.81949ptA\hskip-0.81949ptC\hskip-0.81949ptE}=0
is used.
Vector space
\Omega^{2}
is interpreted as a dual space of
\Omega^{1}
with respect to a bilinear map
\Omega^{1}\otimes\Omega^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}[{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}_{N}]
such that

\displaystyle\langle{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{F}\rangle_{\wedge}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}[{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge}\in\mathbb{R}[{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}_{N}],

where
\Sigma\subset{\mathcal{}M}
is a three-dimensional oriented and compact submanifold of
{\mathcal{}M}
, and
\mathbb{R}[{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}_{N}]
is polynomial algebra of Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}_{N}
with real coefficients.
This bilinear map is non-degenerated and induces homomorphism
\Omega^{2}\cong{\Omega^{1}}^{\#}
; thus,
\mathfrak{F}
is recognized as a one-form object in the space of connections.
Therefore, the existence of the Chern–Simons form is maintained owing to its de Rham cohomology
H_{{d\hskip-0.81949ptR}}^{1}(\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-0.81949pt{\mathfrak{s}}};\delta_{\mathfrak{A}})=0
and the de Rham theorem, where
\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-0.81949pt{\mathfrak{s}}}
is a domain of the Chern–Simons form.
∎

Remark A.8
.

The Chern–Simons form is a closed form with respect to the external derivative such that
d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=0
, and it is consistent with the structure equation
()’
‣
A.2
.

Proof.

The Bianchi identity refBianchihSU2 is represented using the Chern–Simons form as
d\mathfrak{F}+{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=0
, when
\mathfrak{F}
is defined using the structure equation
()’
‣
A.2
.
Therefore, the Chern–Simons form is closed such that
dd\mathfrak{F}+d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=0
with respect to the external derivative in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
.
The external derivative of the Chern–Simons form is directly calculated as

\displaystyle d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}^{I}

\displaystyle=f^{I}_{~JK}\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{K}-{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}\wedge d\mathfrak{F}^{K}\right),

\displaystyle=f^{I}_{~JK}\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{K}+{c}f^{K}_{~LM}{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{L}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{M}\right),

\displaystyle=f^{I}_{~JK}\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}+\frac{{c}}{2}f^{J}_{~ML}{\mathfrak{A}}^{M}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{L}\right)\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{K}=f^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.99997pt\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{J}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{K},

where
\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{J}:=d{\mathfrak{A}}^{J}+{c}f^{J}_{~ML}{\mathfrak{A}}^{M}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{L}/2
.
In this calculaiotn, the Bianchi identity
()’
‣
A.2
and the Jacobi identity are used.
We note that the structure equation
()’
‣
A.2
is not assumed here.
When
\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{J}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{K}
is symmetric in exchange
J
for
K
, relation
d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}^{I}=0
is maintained.
Therefore, closedness of the Chern–Simon form is consistent with
\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{I}=\mathfrak{F}^{I}=d{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 3.00003pt\left[{\mathfrak{A}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}\right]^{I}
.
∎

Whereas
\ref{eq12}\Rightarrow(d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=0)
is trivial, inverse statement
(d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=0)\Rightarrow\ref{eq12}
for given
\mathfrak{F}
is not always true.
When non-singular two-form objects
\mathfrak{F}^{I}
are given, the existence of one-form objects
{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}
fulfilling both
()’
‣
A.2
and
()’
‣
A.2
is discussed here.
The Bianchi identity
()’
‣
A.2
is represented using trivial bases in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
such that

\displaystyle t_{I}\left(\partial_{\circ}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\circ\circ}+{c}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJK}{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{\circ}\mathscr{F}^{K}_{\circ\circ}\right){\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=0,

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\epsilon^{a{\circ}{\circ\circ}}\left(\partial_{\circ}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\circ\circ}+{c}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJK}{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{\circ}\mathscr{F}^{K}_{\circ\circ}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(129)

We note that the flat space-time yields
d{\mathfrak{e}}=0
.
Equation (
129
) is represented using the dual curvature as

\displaystyle\partial_{\circ}[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]^{a{\circ}}+{c}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJK}[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{K}]^{a{\circ}}{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{\circ}=0.

Unknown function
{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{a}
has
N_{\mathscr{A}}:=N_{\textrm{ST}}\times\hskip-1.99997ptN_{SU}
independent components, where
N_{\textrm{ST}}
is a dimension of the space-time manifold and
N_{SU}=N^{2}-1
is total number of freedom for the
SU(N)
symmetry.
Thus, equation (
129
) has
(N_{\mathscr{A}}\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997ptN_{\mathscr{A}})
-matrix representation such that

\displaystyle\left[\partial\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]^{(I;a)}+{c}\left[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]^{(I;a)}_{(J;{\circ})}\left[{\mathscr{A}}\right]^{(J;{\circ})}=0,

(130)

where

\displaystyle\left[\partial\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]^{(I;a)}

\displaystyle:=\eta^{a\star}\eta^{{\circ\circ}}\partial_{\circ}\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}_{\star{\circ}},\hskip 10.00002pt\left[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]^{(I;a)}_{(J;b)}:=\eta^{a{\circ}}f^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJK}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{K}_{{\circ}b},

\displaystyle\left[{\mathscr{A}}\right]^{(J;a)}

\displaystyle:=\eta^{a{\circ}}{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{\circ},

and
(I;a):=N_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt(I\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt1)\hskip-1.99997pt+\hskip-1.99997pta
runs from 1 to
N_{\mathscr{A}}
.
Suppose all
\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\bullet\bullet}
(and thus,
\partial_{\bullet}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\bullet\bullet}
too) are given.
If matrix
[\hat{\mathscr{F}}]
is invertible,
[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}]
is also invertible; thus
{\mathfrak{A}}
is uniquely provided from given
\mathfrak{F}
.
It is proven for the
SU(2)
Yang–Mills theory:

Example A.9
.

Solution of equation
(\ref{bicomp3})
for SU(2)

Matrix
\left[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]
has a block representation such that

\displaystyle\left[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mbox{\boldmath$0$}}&\hskip 10.00002pt\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]&-\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]\\
-\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]&\hskip 10.00002pt{\mbox{\boldmath$0$}}&\hskip 10.00002pt\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]\\
\hskip 10.00002pt\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]&-\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]&\hskip 10.00002pt{\mbox{\boldmath$0$}}\end{array}\right),

where
0
is a
(4\times 4)
zero-matrix.
Here, matrix

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$F$}}}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}&\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]^{-1}&\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]^{-1}\\
\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]^{-1}&{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}&\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]^{-1}\\
\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]^{-1}&\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]^{-1}&{\mbox{\boldmath$1$}}\end{array}\right),

are introduced, where
1
is
(4\times 4)
unit-matrix and
[\bullet]^{-1}
is inverse of matrix
\bullet
.
Notably, all
[\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}]
are assumed to be invertible.
Matrix
\left[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]
is block-diagonalized using
\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$F$}}}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$F$}}}.\left[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]^{-1}\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]&0&0\\
0&\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]^{-1}\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]&0\\
0&0&\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\right]\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{3}\right]^{-1}\left[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{1}\right]\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle~~-\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}(2\leftrightarrow 3)&0&0\\
0&(3\leftrightarrow 1)&0\\
0&0&(1\leftrightarrow 2)\end{array}\right)

Matrix
[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]
are anti-symmetric as
[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]^{T}=-[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]
and
([\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]^{-1})^{T}=-[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]^{-1}
; thus, diagonal block-matrices are symmetric such that

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$F$}}}_{J}

\displaystyle:=[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}][\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{J}]^{-1}[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{K}]-(K\leftrightarrow I),

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$F$}}}_{J}^{T}

\displaystyle=[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{K}]^{T}([\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{J}]^{-1})^{T}[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]^{T}-(K\leftrightarrow I),

\displaystyle=-[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{K}][\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{J}]^{-1}[\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{I}]+(K\leftrightarrow I)=\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$F$}}}_{J}.

Therefore, diagonal blocks of
\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$F$}}}.[f\hat{\mathscr{F}}]
are invertible matrices; thus,
[{\mathscr{A}}]
has a unique solution.

Field matrices
\mathscr{F}^{I}
are anti-symmetric
(N_{\textrm{ST}}\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997ptN_{\textrm{ST}})
-matrices; thus, they have
N_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\times N_{\textrm{ST}}\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt(N_{\textrm{ST}}-1)/2
independent components in total.
On the other hand,
{\mathscr{A}}^{I}
has
(N_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\times N_{\textrm{ST}})
-independent components.
If curvature
\mathfrak{F}
is provided from connection
{\mathfrak{A}}
through the structure equation, all component of
\mathscr{F}^{I}
are not independent; there are
N_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\times N_{\textrm{ST}}\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt(N_{\textrm{ST}}-3)/2
constraints.
In this case, algebraic equation
(\ref{bicomp3})
has a unique solution that fulfils the structure equation.
When arbitrary anti-symmetric matrices
\mathscr{F}^{I}
are given without specifying
{\mathscr{A}}^{I}
, the solution of algebraic equation
(\ref{bicomp3})
does not fulfil
()’
‣
A.2
in general.
A necessary condition to realize a relation
\ref{BianchihSU2}\Rightarrow\ref{eq12}
is provided below.

Theorem A.10
.

Suppose
SU(N)
Lie-algebra valused non-singular two-form object
\mathfrak{F}
given in
\Sigma_{\mathscr{F}}\subseteq{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
is an adjoint representation of
SU(N)
.
Following two statements are equivalent:

1.

\mathfrak{F}
is an
SU(N)
-curvature associated with
SU(N)
-connection
{\mathfrak{A}}
.

2.

Solution of algebraic equation
d\mathfrak{F}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge}=0
with respect to
{\mathfrak{A}}

in compact manifold
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}}
has the vanishing first homology such that;

H_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}},\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}};\mathbb{R})=0
.

Proof.

1.

\Rightarrow

2.
: When
\mathfrak{F}
is a curvature, it is represented using structure equation
\mathfrak{F}=d{\mathfrak{A}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}
and fulfils the Bianchi identity
d\mathfrak{F}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge}=0
.

2.

\Rightarrow

1.
:
When non-singular tensors
\mathscr{F}^{I}_{ab}
for
\mathfrak{F}=\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\circ\circ}({\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}){t_{I}}
are provided, equation
d\mathfrak{F}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge}=0
has a unique solution for
{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{a}
.
When
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}}
has the vanished first homology;
H_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}},\partial\Sigma_{\mathfrak{A}};\mathbb{R})=0
, the statement
2
. equivalently asserts that the exact form
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}:=-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge}
exists in oriented closed
\Sigma_{\widetilde{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-0.81949pt{\mathfrak{s}}}}
corresponding to given
\mathfrak{F}
such that
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=-d\mathfrak{F}
.
Therefore, the first-homology of
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}
is vanished and
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}
trivially induces exact form
d\mathfrak{F}
owing to
Remark
A.1
.
On the other hand, statement
1
. also uniquely induces the Chern–Simons form, which fulfils the Bianchi identity
d\mathfrak{F}+{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}=0
; thus,
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}={{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}
is fulfilled.
Therefore, the theorem is maintained.
∎

A.3
SU(N)
gauge group in curved space-time

Finally, non-abelian gauge of
SU(N)
group in the curved space-time is considered.
Exactness and closedness are represented, respectively, such that:

\displaystyle\emph{Covariant\hskip 5.0ptexactness}\hskip 3.00003pt:

\displaystyle\hskip 13.00005pt\mathfrak{F}=d{\mathfrak{A}}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}{\mathfrak{w}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}},\vskip 2.84526pt

(131a)

\displaystyle\emph{Covariant\hskip 5.0ptclosedness}:

\displaystyle\hskip 1.00006ptd_{G}\mathfrak{F}=d\mathfrak{F}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}[{\mathfrak{w}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge}=0.

(131b)

A component representation of, e.g.,
{\mathfrak{w}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}
, using the trivial bases in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathcal{}M}}
is provided as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{w}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle=[{\mathfrak{w}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}]_{ab}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{b}=\left(\eta_{b{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega_{\mu}^{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ}\star}{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{\star}{\mathcal{E}}^{\mu}_{a}\right)\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\hskip-1.00006pt\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{b}\hskip 1.99997ptt_{I}.

Here, an approximation that spin form
{\mathfrak{w}}
is a external field that has constant values independent from
{\mathfrak{A}}
and
\mathfrak{F}
, is exploited.
In this case, the Chern–Simons form is defied as

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left({\mathfrak{w}},{\mathfrak{A}}\right):=[{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}{\mathfrak{w}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{F}]_{\wedge},

and thus, its closedness with respect to the external derivative is maintained;
d{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left({\mathfrak{w}},{\mathfrak{A}}\right)=0
.
Anti-derivative operator
\delta_{{\mathfrak{w}}\otimes{\mathfrak{A}}}
is defined similarly as (
127
), and the exactness with respect to the anti-derivative operator
\delta_{{\mathfrak{w}}\otimes{\mathfrak{A}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{{\mathfrak{c}}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathfrak{s}}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left({\mathfrak{w}},{\mathfrak{A}}\right)=\mathfrak{F}
is maintained.

Theorem
A.10
is also maintained in this case because spin form
{\mathfrak{w}}
is assumed to be constant valued.

A.4
Existence of dual spinor

It is known that existence of a spin structure in a bundle is distinguished owing to the first and second Stiefel–Whitney classes
[
52
]
:

Remark A.11
.

Suppose
w^{i}(M)\in H^{i}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is
i^{th}
Stiefel–Whitney class of orientable manifold
M
.

1.

Manifold
M
has a spin structure, if and only if

\displaystyle w^{2}(M)\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong Hom(H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\mathbb{Z}_{2})

vanishes;
w^{2}(M)=0
.

2.

When
M
has a spin structure, each spin structure has one-to-one correspondence to

\displaystyle H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong Hom(\pi_{1}(M),\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

where
\pi_{1}(M)
is fundamental group of
M
.

Proof.

For a proof of the remark, see, e.g., chapter 4, section 5 in Ref.
[
53
]

∎

For a three-dimensional manifold
{\mathcal{}M}^{3}_{\overline{r=0}}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\{r=0\}
, any closed surface
\Sigma^{2}_{\overline{r=0}}\subset{\mathcal{}M}^{3}_{\overline{r=0}}
such that
[\Sigma^{2}_{\overline{r=0}}]\in H_{2}({\mathcal{}M}^{3}_{\overline{r=0}},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is homologous to
S^{2}
, and any vector bundle in
S^{2}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(SO(3))
.
Therefore, both first and second Stiefel–Whitney classes vanish, and only one spin structure exists in
{\mathcal{}M}^{3}_{\overline{r=0}}
.
As shown in
Example
5.1
, the Coulomb type potential is defined in
\mathbb{R}\otimes{\mathcal{}M}^{3}_{\overline{r=0}}
; thus, there exists a spinorial section corresponding to an electric monopole in the Yang–Mills theory with
U(1)
-gauge group (an electron in electromagnetic theory).
On the other hand, a manifold
{\mathcal{}M}^{3}_{\overline{x=y=0}}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\{x=y=0\}
has a non-trivial two-dimensional surface such as a torus winding around the Dirac string.
Therefore, the second Stiefel–Whitney class does not vanish;
w^{2}({\mathcal{}M}^{3}_{\overline{x=y=0}})\cong\mathbb{Z}
; thus, the spin structure does not exist in a dual-bundle.
Therefore, in a space with an isolated magnetic monopole, an electron does not exist.
If a pair of magnetic monopoles with opposite charges makes a magnetic dipole, the second Stiefel–Whitney class is trivial; thus, a spin structure exists.
